## Page 1


Categorical Lusztig cycles and weave schobers
Roger Casals and Merlin Christ
Abstract
We establish the foundations of categorical weave calculus, developing the diagrammatic calculus
of weaves and braid varieties within the study of Calabi-Yau triangulated categories and cluster
tilting theory. This is achieved by associating a perverse sheaf of triangulated categories to each
Demazure weave. A central contribution is the construction and study of the categorical Lusztig
cycles and their duals, which we show form simple-minded and silting collections in the category
of global sections of such a sheaf of categories. These categorical collections are built using the
diagrammatics of weaves and we study their behavior under changes of weaves. For instance, we
show that they undergo tilts under weave mutations. En route, we develop the study of categorical
weighted braid words, as canonical rigid filtered dg modules over derived preprojective algebras,
and the categorical incarnation of the tropical Lusztig rules, as a gluing mechanism for such filtered
objects. Appendix A also contains homological results, providing a novel construction of simple-
minded and silting collections from full exceptional collections, and characterizing when these arise
from a highest weight structure on an abelian category.

Introduction
The object of this article is to establish the foundations of categorical weave calculus. This allows for
many of the applications of weave calculus and braid varieties to be elevated to the study of relative
Calabi-Yau categories and cluster tilting theory. We first associate certain perverse schobers to De-
mazure weaves, which we refer to as weave schobers. A central contribution is then the construction
and study of the categorical Lusztig cycles, which are objects in the triangulated category of global
section of such a weave schober and generalize the combinatorial Lusztig weave cycles. We show that
the categorical Lusztig cycles of a weave schober form a simple-minded collection, are invariant under
weave equivalence, and undergo a simple tilt under weave mutation. En route, we also introduce and
develop the study of categorical weighted braid words, as canonical filtered objects in the derived cat-
egory of the preprojective algebra, the categorical incarnation of the tropical Lusztig rules, as a gluing
mechanism for such filtered objects, and the relation between mutations of exceptional collections and
2


## Page 3


mutations of their simple-minded collections.
We also establish corresponding results for the dual categorical Lusztig cycles, which form a silting
collection Koszul dual to the categorical Lusztig cycles, and construct explicit weave realizations. In
a nutshell, we provide a complete foundational set of results enhancing weave calculus and the study
of braid varieties to the context of 3-Calabi–Yau categories and cluster tilting theory. In addition, the
results on weave schobers established in this article have a number of applications, some of which will
be further explored in subsequent work. These include the construction of additive categorifications
for the cluster algebras on braid varieties, and a form of homological mirror symmetry between the
Fukaya-Seidel categories associated to a weave and the non-commutative crepant resolutions associated
to weave quivers with potentials.
1.1
Scientific context
Weaves were introduced in [CZ22] within the context of symplectic topology.
They have since
been developed in different directions, including ones within combinatorial and algebraic frameworks,
cf. e.g. [CGGS24, CGG+25b]. In this context, the study of weaves can be understood as a planar
diagrammatic calculus, with certain moves & mutations between weaves, and the combinatorics of
their cycles and associated functions on braid varieties.
See Figure 1 for two instances of weaves
related by weave mutations, and their Lusztig cycles.
The basic results on the diagrammatics of
weaves, including their moves & mutations, and the Lusztig cycles and their intersection theory, are
summarized in [CGG+25b, Section 4], see also [CGGS24,CW24,CZ22]. This collection of results and
diagrammatic techniques on weaves, which is centered around the Lusztig cycles, is often referred to
as weave calculus, cf. e.g. Section 2.1.
Figure 1: Two instances of Demazure weaves w, w′ : β −→β′ from β = s2s1(s1s2)3 ∈Br+
3
to β′ = s2s1s2, where blue weave edges are labeled by s1 ∈S3 and red weave edges by
s2 ∈S3. (Left) The mutable Lusztig cycles γ1, γ2, γ3 for w are highlighted in yellow, green
and pink respectively, and their intersection quiver is displayed in the square box at the
lower right of w. (Right) The weave w′ obtained from w by performing weave mutations
along the Lusztig cycles γ3 and γ2, in this order. Its mutable Lusztig cycles γ′

also highlighted and their intersection quiver is displayed to the lower left of w′.
Weave calculus has been successfully used to tackle different problems, some of which include
the construction of cluster algebras on braid varieties [CGG+25b, CGGS24, CGG+25a], a proof that
the Muller-Speyer twist is Donaldson-Thomas and that source and target positroids seeds quasi-
coincide [CLSBW23], the Floer theoretic characterization of Gaiotto-Moore-Neitzke spectral networks
3


## Page 4


[CN25,GMN13,GMN14], and the microlocal study of Legendrian links [CW24,CL22,CL26]. In inter-
esting cases, including all finite and affine Dynkin types, all cluster seeds and the cluster automorphism
groups can be realized by weaves [ABL25,Hug23a,Hug23b], and see also [ACSH+25,CSHW25,HR25]
for more applications of weave calculus. We emphasize that in all these results, the study of the
Lusztig cycles for the corresponding weaves is a key ingredient, as it provides all the necessary data
for the cluster seeds, including the quivers and both types of cluster variables.
The main goal of this article is to develop the foundations of categorical weave calculus, which
is centered around the categorical Lusztig cycles. The applications above hint towards the existence
of such a diagrammatic categorical calculus, but at the same time indicate that its development
requires new ideas and techniques. Indeed, on the one hand, such calculus would provide a diagram-
matic presentation of many interesting Fukaya-Seidel categories and parts of their spaces of stability
conditions, functorially interact with the additive cluster categorifications, and generalize the categori-
fication of higher-rank Fock-Goncharov theory of local systems, cf. [Chr25a,KL25], to irregular Betti
surfaces. On the other hand, at a technical level, it should provide a categorical lift of the tropical
Lusztig moves for weighted braid words, the intersection theory of weave cycles in Demazure weaves
from [CZ22,CW24,CGG+25b], and feature a triangulated counterpart of the theory of highest weight
abelian categories.
The manuscript addresses many of these aspects by developing new ideas and techniques.
These
include the construction of weave perverse schobers, the study of rigid filtered objects associated to
weighted braid words, and new homological insights in the context of k-linear stable ∞-categories,
such as the categorical lift of the tropical Lusztig rules in terms of the propagation of sections of weaves
schobers, and the relation between mutations of exceptional collections and mutations for their asso-
ciated simple-minded and silting collections.
Regarding the technical implementation of such ideas, the categorical weave calculus we present
uses the formalism of perverse schobers, introduced in [KS14]. Intuitively, the perverse schobers we
use are a certain class of constructible sheaves of k-linear stable ∞-categories on a surface. Specif-
ically, we use the theory of perverse schobers parametrized by ribbon graphs embedded in surfaces,
introduced in [Chr22b, Sections 3&4], [CHQ23, Section 3], see also Section 2.2 below, which provides
the key ingredients on perverse schobers needed to develop our results.
1.2
The main results
A central point illustrated by our results is that
There exists a categorical enhancement of weave calculus, constructed using perverse schobers. In it,
Lusztig cycles and their calculus have canonical enhancements to a relative CY3 category.
As evidence, we now highlight three of our main results, as follows.
(A) Let us first start with the categorical enhancement of the Lusztig cycles associated to a
Demazure weave w. By definition, a weighted braid word (β, a) is a pair consisting of a positive braid
word β = si0 · · · sil and a collection of non-negative integers a := (a0, . . . , al), where sj denotes the
jth Artin generator of the braid group in Type A, i.e. corresponding to the jth vertex of the Dynkin
diagram. First, as summarized in Section 2.1.2, each non-categorical Lusztig cycle of a weave w can
be understood as a certain sequence of weighted braid words (β1, a1) →· · · →(βk, ak) whose weights
propagate downwards through w using the following tropical Lusztig rules:
Φ1 : (a1, a2) 7→min(a1, a2),
Φ2 : (a1, a2) 7→(a2, a1),
Φ3 : (a1, a2, a3) 7→(a2 + a3 −min(a1, a3), min(a1, a3), a1 + a2 −min(a1, a3)) ,
(1)

Φ3 at hexavalent vertices, where the braid word changes via sisjsi = sjsisj with i, j adjacent.
Therefore, there are two tasks to obtain the right notion of a categorical Lusztig cycle:
(i) The first task is to construct the categorical version of a weighted braid word (β, a). This is
achieved in Section 5, where we construct a canonical rigid filtered object Lβ,a associated to any
weighted braid word (β, a) arising from Lusztig cycles:
∃! rigid filtered object Lβ,a ∈D whose graded pieces recover (β, a)
(2)
This is established in Theorem 5.1, which refines the above statement. Specifically, the object
Lβ,a belongs to the derived ∞-category D = Dperf(Π2(An)) of the additive derived preprojective
algebra of type An. The existence and uniqueness of the object Lβ,a is a novel result within the
representation theory of Π2(An). For its construction, we will rely on a partial geometric model
for D in terms of matching 2-spheres and matching paths in the disc, which allows us to express
Lβ,a as a sequence of resolutions for the intersections of matching paths.
(ii) The second task is to use the sequence (β1, a1) →· · · →(βk, ak) associated to a Lusztig cycle
to glue these filtered objects Lβi,ai together into an object of a certain category. This should be
done in a manner that the tropical Lusztig rules displayed in Equation (1) are recovered when
considering graded pieces. This step requires constructing such a category from a Demazure
weave w first: we achieve this in Section 3 by constructing a perverse schober Fw associated to
a weave w. In this context, the required gluing of the filtered objects is achieved by establishing
certain fiber and cofiber sequences between them, as discussed in Section 4. For instance, if
(βi, ai) →(βi+1, ai+1) is a trivalent vertex, Proposition 6.5 will establish a cofiber sequence
At a trivalent: τ≥0 Mor(Sp, Lβi,ai) ⊗Sp −→Lβi,ai −→Lβi+1,ai+1

At a non-trivalent vertex: Lβi,ai ≃Lβi+1,ai+1

The equivalences in (4) are of the underlying objects, which suffices to glue, but the filtrations
themselves are typically different. Such equivalences are established in Proposition 6.2. The
result of such gluings, using (2), (3) and (4), is so that the categorical Lusztig cycles shall be
objects in the category of global sections RΓ(Gw, Fw), cf. Definition 4.1, which will be a smooth
and proper k-linear stable ∞-category with a relative 3-Calabi–Yau structure.
In a nutshell, these constructions in Sections 3, 4 and 5 show that categorical Lusztig cycles exist
and abide by a categorical enhancement of the tropical Lusztig propagation rules for weighted braid
words. Though establishing these results requires developing new results that might be of interest on
their own, as illustrated by the content of these sections, the first main result can be summarized as
follows:

Theorem A (Categorical Lusztig cycles). Let w : β →δ(β) be a Demazure weave with m trivalent
vertices and Fw its weave schober. Then:
(1) Let (β, a) be a weighted braid word arising from a horizontal slice of a Lusztig cycle of w.
Then, up to equivalence, there exists a unique (β, a)-filtered object whose colimit Lβ,a ∈D is
rigid. In addition, its graded pieces uniquely recover the weighted braid word (β, a).
(2) Let (βi, ai) →(βi+1, ai+1) be the two weighted braid words arising from a horizontal slice of a
Lusztig cycle of w above and under a vertex p ∈Gw. Then

Theorem A is proven in Sections 4, 5 and 6, once the weave schober Fw is introduced and studied in
Section 3. Three comments on Theorem A for now:
(i) We also show that the rigid geometric realization Lβ,a in Theorem A.(1) belongs to the subcat-
egory M ⊆D of matching objects, thus giving a geometric model for Lβ,a, cf. Section 5.
(ii) The morphism in (5), whose cofiber measures how the objects Lβ,a propagate through a trivalent
vertex of w, is the non-negative truncation τ≥0(ev) of the evaluation map of the derived Hom. Its
cofiber categorically represents a truncated spherical twist, referred to as a semi-twist. Different
versions of semi-twists are ubiquitous in categorical weave calculus, as they categorically capture
the tropical propagation rules for weave cycles.
(iii) Intuitively, Theorem A.(3) states that the canonical rigid objects Lβ1,a1, . . . , Lβk,ak arising via
Theorem A.(1) from scanning a given Lusztig cycle of w, can be glued together to a global
section Lw
i ∈RΓ(Gw, Fw) of the weave schober Fw. The equivalences in Theorem A.(2) are a
key part of this gluing and the well-definedness of the resulting categorical Lusztig cycles.
(B) Let us now discuss the categorical enhancement of weave calculus. Theorem A provides the
categorical Lusztig cycles, with the basic properties that indeed make them categorical avatars of the
classical Lusztig weave cycles. A central feature of weave calculus are the notions of weave equivalences
and weave mutations. These are summarized in Section 2.1.1, see particularly Figures 4 and 5. In
this regard, the first task to start developing a categorical weave calculus is to ensure that:
(i) The relevant categories, objects and properties associated to a Demazure weave w should be
invariant under weave equivalences. In particular, the category RΓ(Gw, Fw) and its categorical
Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }i∈[1,m] featuring in Theorem A should be invariant under weave equivalences.
(ii) There must be a categorical enhancement for weave mutation, so that we have precise formu-
las describing how the relevant categories, objects and homological properties associated to a
weave w change under weave mutations. In particular, we should understand how the category
RΓ(Gw, Fw) and its categorical Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }i∈[1,m] change under weave mutations.
6


## Page 7


We will establish these two items throughout the manuscript, laying the foundations for a categor-
ical weave calculus. Specifically, though the weave schober Fw itself is not directly a weave invariant,
we show in Section 3 that its ∞-category of global sections RΓ(Gw, Fw) is invariant under both weave
equivalences and weave mutations, cf. Theorem 3.8. The behavior of categorical Lusztig cycles is
harder to establish, cf. Section 8, but the outcome is neat:
The categorical Lusztig cycles form a simple-minded collection in RΓ(Gw, Fw), and are invariant
under weave equivalences. Plus, weave mutations induce mutations of such simple-minded collections.
The proof that the categorical Lusztig cycles form a simple-minded collection, in the sense of Defini-
tion A.1.(4), is contentful. It requires us to construct certain exceptional collections in RΓ(Gw, Fw)
from which {Lw
i }i∈[1,m] can be constructed, which we do in Section 7 and Section 8.1. The relation
between the exceptional collection and the categorical Lusztig cycles is similar to the relation between
the standard objects and the simple objects in a highest weight abelian category. (The precise relation
to the theory of highest weight categories is explored in Appendix A.) Nevertheless these exceptional
collections are not invariant under weave equivalence and thus, in a sense, are not well-defined from
the viewpoint of weave calculus. We overcome this fact in Sections 8.2 and 8.3 by studying how mu-
tations of exceptional collections affect the associated simple-minded collections, which can be seen as
a homological result of interest on its own.
At core, the second result establishing a categorical weave calculus can be summarized as follows:
Theorem B (Categorical weave calculus). Let w : β →δ(β) be a Demazure weave and Fw its
weave schober. Then the following holds:
(1) The stable ∞-category of global sections RΓ(Gw, Fw) is invariant under weave equivalence
and weave mutation. In particular, it is an invariant of the boundary braid β.
(2) The categorical Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }i∈[1,m] form a simple-minded collection in RΓ(Gw, Fw).
(3) Let w ∼w′ be a weave equivalence. Then the collections of categorical Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }
and {Lw′
i } coincide, up to a permutation of the indices i ∈[1, m].
(4) Let w →w′ be a forward weave mutation. Then {Lw′
i } is obtained from {Lw
i } by a forward
mutation of simple-minded collections.
Theorem B is established in Section 8.
Note that Theorem B.(4) implies that {Lw
i } undergoes a
backward mutation of simple-minded collections under a backward weave mutation of w. In a nut-
shell, Theorem B establishes that {Lw
i }, built via Theorem A, form a simple-minded collection in
RΓ(Gw, Fw), well-defined for the weave equivalence class of w, and weave mutations lead to a form of
tilting for {Lw
i }.
(C) Finally, we also introduce and develop the dual notion of categorical Lusztig cycles in Sec-
tion 9, which we refer to as categorical dual Lusztig cycles. Note that the notion of dual Lusztig cycles
is new even within non-categorical weave calculus, as no weave cycles for the cluster A-variables were
introduced in [CGG+25b].1 Since the Lusztig cycles propagate downwards through the weave and are,
coarsely said, as small as possible, the dual Lusztig cycles must propagate the weave upwards and be
as large as possible, e.g. they never have compact support.
1Intuitively, Lusztig cycles yield cluster X-variables and dual Lusztig cycles correspond to cluster A-variables. Cat-
egorically, the categorical Lusztig cycles additively categorify the X-variables, and their categorical duals additively
categorify the A-variables, via the cluster tilting object.

theory in the context of stable ∞-categories of global sections of perverse schobers, see Lemmas 9.9
and 9.10 or the discussion in Section 9.4.

1.3
A comment on applications
Theorems A, B, C, and the new techniques developed to prove them are a core contribution of this
manuscript, establishing the foundations of categorical weave calculus. At the same time, these con-
tributions lead to a number of new results and applications. Indeed, in the same manner that the
non-categorical weave calculus leads to new results in a variety of contexts, cf. Section 1.1 above, the
uses of this categorical enhancement are manifold. In particular:
(i) Symplectic topology. A symplectic geometric realization of the cluster algebras constructed
in [CGG+25b] stems from the study of Lagrangian fillings of Legendrian links, see e.g. [CW24,CZ22].
This is a geometric problem about certain surfaces in 4-dimensions, and a Demazure weave w yields
such a Lagrangian filling. Intuitively, the cluster algebra is the coordinate ring of functions for a
moduli of Lagrangian fillings, with different Lagrangian fillings (e.g. coming from Demazure weaves)
providing the cluster charts. It is much desirable to have a symplectic realization for categorical weave
calculus, e.g. for the weave schober Fw, its saturated relative 3-Calabi–Yau category RΓ(Gw, Fw), and
the categorical Lusztig cycles and their duals.
For that, we shall first construct a symplectic 6-manifold (Yw, λw) with a Lefschetz fibration
π : Yw −→C such that RΓ(Gw, Fw) will be the partially wrapped Fukaya category associated to
π.
Then we will explain how (Yw, λw) relates to the Lagrangian fillings in 4-dimensions by using
9


## Page 10


matching 2-disks and the L-compressing systems from [CG24]. In particular, this yields a rigorous
symplectic geometric model for these cluster algebras, from the 4-dimensional study of Lagrangian
fillings, and for the cluster categories, here obtained via the Fukaya-Seidel category of a 6-dimensional
manifold, with a geometric map connecting these two models. In this symplectic context, we shall ex-
plain the meaning of the categorical Lusztig cycles in Theorem A, in terms of Lagrangian skeleta, of the
dual ones in Theorem C, in terms of Lagrangian cocores, and of the cluster algebra from [CGG+25b]
as it relates to the Fukaya-Seidel category.
(ii) Quivers with potential. By combining [CG24, Section 2] and [CGG+25b, Section 7.4], there
is a canonical ice quiver with potential (Qw, Ww) associated to a Demazure weave w. We shall es-
tablish a relation between the ∞-category of global sections RΓ(Gw, Fw) of the weave schober Fw,
and the perfectly valued derived ∞-category of the relative 3-Calabi–Yau Ginzburg dg-algebra of such
(Qw, Ww). The relative Ginzburg algebra is obtained from the categorical dual Lusztig cycles {Pw
i },
and its simple modules identify with the categorical Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }. In particular, the description
in terms of the quiver potential shows that the weave cycle intersection pairing of the Lusztig cycles,
see [CGG+25b, Sections 4.5&4.6], computes the Euler characteristics of the Ext-groups between the
categorical Lusztig cycles. We will also prove that the Donaldson-Thomas transformation of the quiver
can be realized via a sequence of weave mutations and that, in categorical weave calculus, the associ-
ated transformation coincides with the Ringel-type duality in (7).
(iii) Additive categorifications of cluster algebras.
The results of this manuscript lead to
additive categorifications of the cluster algebras associated to braid varieties. In the reduced case
β = w0w, w ∈W a reduced word, such additive categorifications have been previously studied via
the abelian categories of modules over preprojective algebras and their exact subcategories, see for
instance [GLS06,GLS08,BIRS09]. Our contributions relate to these previous works as follows.
First, we tackle the general case where β is any braid word with δ(β) = w0, not necessarily the
reduced case. The silting collections we construct yield cluster tilting objects in the Higgs category or,
equivalently by [Wu23, Ex. 8.19], in the module category of the preprojective algebra. These cluster
tilting objects generalize those associated to the maximal rigid modules from [GLS06,GLS08,BIRS09].
The so-called layer modules in these works are a special case of the 2-spherical objects associated with
the trivalent vertices of the weave, see Definition 3.1 and Remark 5.3. In this sense, the filtrations of
the maximal rigid modules by layer modules, cf. [IR11, Cor. 2.9], are generalized to arbitrary weaves
via the filtrations in Theorem A and Theorem C.(3). In addition, [GLS07, GLS11, IR11] show that
the endomorphism algebra of the cluster tilting object in the reduced case is quasi-hereditary, i.e. its
module category is a highest weight abelian category. The triangulated categories constructed in our
paper thus generalize the derived categories of these quasi-hereditary algebras. We allow the associ-
ated category to be triangulated, not just abelian, and equip them with a full exceptional collection,
in place of a highest weight structure, cf. Section 9.4 and Appendix A.
Second, even in the reduced case and for a given reduced expression, we construct cluster tilting
objects corresponding to many cluster seeds, not just one. Specifically, we recover the seed associated
to a maximal rigid module by considering the right-inductive weave. The above discussions concern
Type A, and details will appear in [CC26]. We expect this relation to hold in the case of a general
finite Dynkin diagram, cf. Remark 3.7.
Remark 1.1. Relatedly, in the context of local systems with irregular singularities, non-categorical
weave calculus allows for the construction of cluster algebras for the coordinate rings of wild character
varieties, see e.g. [FG06, Section 1.15] and [CZ22, Section 3], or [CN25]. Similar to (2) above, there is
an important dissonance between the real 4-dimensional geometry that leads to the non-abelianization
maps of [GMN13,GMN14] and the cluster algebras in [CW24,CGG+25b], versus the real 6-dimensional
geometry that leads to the 3-Calabi–Yau categorical techniques used to categorify such cluster algebras,
10


## Page 11


see e.g. [Gon17, Section 6] or [Chr22b, Chr25a]. The categorical weave calculus developed here, in
conjunction with application (i) above, resolves this picture by providing a geometric factorization
of the appropriate symplectic 3-Calabi–Yau manifold as a high-dimensional branched cover over the
symplectic push-off of the Lagrangian filling associated to w, explaining geometrically how these two
approaches are related.
Finally, putting (i)-(ii) above together yields a form of homological mirror symmetry, established
through the global sections RΓ(Gw, Fw) of the weave schober Fw: in the A-side there is a Fukaya-
Seidel category and its associates, such as the relative Rabinowitz category, and in the B-side there
is the relative Ginzburg dg-algebra and its associates, such as the cluster category or Higgs category,
cf. [Wu23, Corollary 5.26].
Here the A-side is readily symplectic, whereas these categories in the
B-side are understood as a form of non-commutative crepant resolutions. These further results and
applications of categorical weave calculus will be established in [CC26].
Organization of the paper: The manuscript proves the above results as follows. Section 2 provides
the necessary ingredients on weaves and perverse schobers, in Section 2.1 and Section 2.2 respectively.
The weave schober Fw in Theorems A and B is constructed in Section 3.1 and Theorem B.(1) is proven
in Section 3.2, cf. Theorem 3.8. The categorical Lusztig cycles are constructed in Section 4, using
results latter established in Section 6. Theorem A is also proven in Section 6. The proofs in Section 6
base on results and techniques on categorical weighted braid words developed in Section 5. Theorem
B.(2), (3) and (4) are then proven in Section 8, using the constructions from Section 7, and Theorem
C in Section 9. Finally, discussions on the relation with the theory of highest weight abelian categories
can be found in Section 9.4 and Appendix A.

Notation on ∞-categories: We use the theory of stable ∞-categories as developed in [Lur09,Lur17].
The ∞-category of small ∞-categories is denoted by Cat∞, as in [Lur09, Chapter 3]. The ∞-category of
small stable ∞-categories is denoted St, cf. [Lur17, Section 1.1.4]. All stable ∞-categories appearing in
this paper will be small. We denote by Stidem ⊂St the full subcategory of idempotent complete stable
∞-categories. Ind-completion defines an equivalence between Stidem and the ∞-category of compactly
generated, presentable ∞-categories and compact objects preserving, left adjoint functors PrL,ω
St . The
symmetric monoidal ∞-structure on PrL,ω
St
thus induces a symmetric monoidal structure on Stidem.
Given a field k, Dperf(k) ∈Stidem defines an algebra object and we define LinCatsm
k
:= ModDperf(k)(St)
to be the ∞-category of small, idempotent complete, k-linear stable ∞-categories. Note that the
forgetful functor LinCatsm
k
→Stidem preserves limits by [Lur17, Cor. 3.4.3.2]. All limits computed
in Stidem in this paper will be preserved by the forgetful functor Stidem →St, as a k-linear stable
∞-category with a full exceptional collection is automatically idempotent complete. The forgetful
functor St →Cat∞also preserves limits.

in degree i, as in [Lur17]. In particular, for the non-negative truncation functor τ≥0, which we use

2
Preliminary Ingredients
This section introduces the necessary ingredients to establish the results in this manuscript. Specif-
ically, Section 2.1 provides a summarizing account of non-categorical weave calculus, including the
definition of weave equivalences and mutations, and the non-categorical Lusztig cycles. Section 2.2
recalls the notion of a perverse schober parametrized by a ribbon graph and provides a few facts about
perverse schobers that we use throughout the article.
Figure 2: A Demazure weave w : β −→δ(β) from the 3-stranded braid word β =
s2s1(s1s2)3 to β′ = s2s1s2, where blue weave edges are labeled by s1 ∈S3 and red
weave edges by s2 ∈S3. Generic horizontal slices are depicted in yellow dashed lines,
and the positive braid word associated to each such slice is written to its right. We have
highlighted in purple the letters of each braid word to which the next move will be applied
when scanning the Demazure weave downward.
2.1
A primer on weave calculus
The focus of the article will be on Demazure weaves, whose non-categorical weave calculus we now
briefly summarize. For specificity, we focus on Type An, though similar considerations apply to all
types, cf. [CGG+25b, Section 4]. Let β, β′ be two positive braid words on n-strands, i.e. words on
the positive Artin generators s1, . . . , sn−1 of the braid group on n-strands. By definition, a Demazure
weave w : β −→β′ is a sequence of positive braid words β →β1 →β2 →· · · →β′ such that each
move βj →βj+1 is either a braid move in the positive braid monoid or the move s2
i →si. Figure 2
depicts an example of a Demazure weave w diagrammatically. Specifically, by drawing a positive braid
word si1 · · · sil as a set of parallel vertical strands on the plane, with the jth strand labeled by sij,
these moves can be depicted diagrammatically as in Figure 3. A core aspect of weave calculus are
the Lusztig cycles of a Demazure weave, their intersection theory, and their behavior under weave
equivalences and mutations, which we will momentarily summarize.
These diagrams for Demazure weaves are always read top-to-bottom, the diagram itself is often re-
ferred to as a weave. Considered as a planar graph, a Demazure weave admits the three types of
vertices as in Figure 3, and the connected components of the complement of the vertices are said to be
weave edges, cf. [CW24, Definition 2.1] or [CZ22, Section 2] for the general definition. The trivalent
12


## Page 13


Figure 3: The three types of vertices allowed in the diagrammatic version of a weave w.
(Left) Trivalent vertex, where all edges are decorated with the same permutation si ∈Sn.
It represents the move s2
i →si. (Center) Hexavalent vertex, with the edge decorations
alternating between si, sj with |i −j| = 1. It represents the braid move sisjsi →sjsisj.
(Right) Tetravalent vertex, with edge decorations as drawn with |i −k| > 1. It represents
the braid move sisk = sksi.
vertices of a weave w will be denoted p1, . . . , pm, ordered bottom-to-top, e.g. with pi at height i. Each
horizontal slice not intersecting a vertex determines a positive braid word by reading the weave edges
left-to-right, as illustrated in Figure 2.
Figure 4: A few weave equivalences, in all of them blue indicates s1 ∈S3, red s2, and
green s3. (i) This weave equivalence encodes a syzygy between braid moves, it is also
known as the Zamolodchikov relation.
(ii) A weave equivalence known as the push-
through move, as it allows for pushing trivalent vertices through hexavalent vertices. (iii)
A weave equivalence allowing for distant colors to pass through vertices of each other.
2.1.1
Weave equivalence and mutations
The notion of weave mutation was introduced in [CZ22, Section 4.8]. Equivalences between weaves,
also known as moves, were discussed in [CZ22, Theorem 1.1]. See also [CGGS24, Section 4]. We
consider the following equivalence relation on weaves:
(i) Two weaves w, w′ : β →β′ consisting only of braid moves are declared to be equivalent, see
e.g. Figure 4.(i).
(ii) If |i −j| = 1, the weaves w : sisjsisj →sjsisjsj →sjsisj and w′ : sisjsisj →sisisjsi →
sisjsi →sjsisj are declared to be equivalent, cf. Figure 4.(ii).
(iii) If |i −j| ≥2, the weaves w : sisjsi →sisisj →sisj →sjsi and sisjsi →sjsisi →sjsi are
declared to be equivalent, cf. Figure 4.(iii).
13


## Page 14


By definition, two weaves w, w′ : β →β′ are said to be equivalent if there is a sequence of local
equivalences as above starting at w and ending at w′. Note that the equivalences in (ii) and (iii) are
parameterized by rank 2 subdiagrams of types A2 and A1 × A1 respectively.
Figure 5: (Left) An elementary weave mutation, between the only two Demazure weaves
from w, w′ : s3
1 →s1. (Right) A more elaborate form of weave mutation, obtained by
composing weave equivalences with an elementary weave mutation.
By definition, the two weaves w, w′ : s3
i →si are said to be are related by an elementary weave
mutation, cf. Figure 5 (left) or Figure 6. They are not considered to be weave equivalent. In general,
two weaves are said to be related by a weave mutation if they can be related by a sequence of
weave equivalences, an elementary weave mutation, and another sequence of weave mutations. See for
instance cf. Figure 5 (right). It is proven in [CGGS24, Theorem 4.6] that any two Demazure weaves
w, w′ : β →δ(β) are related by a sequence of equivalence moves and elementary mutations, where we
have fixed a braid word for the Demazure product δ(β) of β.
Figure 6: A redrawing of Figure 5 (left), with new notation to specify direction. Recall
that a weave mutation is given by a sequence of weave equivalences, one local exchange
of the weave according to the two diagrams depicted (with any color si), and another
sequence of weave equivalences. In a forward weave mutation, the piece of weave on the
left is exchanged by the piece of weave on the right. A backward weave mutation is the
inverse process.
In order to match the notation used in the tilting theory of finite length hearts, we specifically distin-
guish the direction of a weave mutation by using the adjectives forward and backward according to
Figure 6.
2.1.2
Weighted braid words and Lusztig propagation rules
Let w : β →β′ be a Demazure weave and E(w) its set of weave edges. By definition, a weave cycle on
w is a function γ : E(w) →Z≥0 that assigns a non-negative integer to each edge of the weave. The
values of γ are said to be the weights of the edges for the cycle w, cf. [CGG+25b, Section 4.4].
14


## Page 15


In the same manner that each horizontal slice of a weave yields a braid word si1 · · · sik, cf. Figure 2,
given a weave cycle γ and a horizontal slice we obtain a braid word si1 · · · sik and a collection of non-
negative integers a := (a1, . . . , ak), where aj := γ(eij) is the weight of the jth weave edge eij ∈E(w)
being intersected in that horizontal slice, corresponding to the letter sij in the braid word si1 · · · sik.
By definition, we refer to such a pair (si1 · · · sik, a) as a weighted braid word. Of particular importance
are the following weave cycles associated to the trivalent vertices of the weave w:
Definition 2.1 (Lusztig cycles). Let w be a Demazure weave and p ∈w a trivalent vertex. By
definition, the Lusztig cycle γp associated to p is the unique weave cycle γp : E(W) →Z≥0 such that:
(i) γp(es) = 1 for the unique south edge es at the trivalent vertex p, and γp(e) = 0 if e is any weave
edge that contains any points above p.
(ii) At any other trivalent vertex with top edges e1, e2 and south edge e, γp satisfies
γp(e) = min(γp(e1), γp(e2)) .
(10)

3) = γp(e2) + γp(e1) −min(γp(e1), γp(e3)) .

Definition 2.1 can be also described in terms of weighted braid words as follows. To construct γp,
first consider a horizontal weave slice right below the height of p, which yields a certain braid word
β(p) := si1 · · · sik(p) and assign the weights a(p) := (0, 0, . . . , 0, 1, 0, . . . , 0), where the only non-zero
weight 1 is assigned to the weave edge south of p. Then, propagate this weighted braid word (β(p), a(p))
downwards by using the following rules for the weights:
Φ1 : (a1, a2) 7→min(a1, a2),
Φ2 : (a1, a2) 7→(a2, a1),

(11)
where Φ1 is used at trivalent vertices, where the braid word changes according to s2

tetravalent vertices, where the braid word changes as sisk = sksi with i, k non-adjacent, and Φ3 at
hexavalent vertices, where the braid word changes via sisjsi = sjsisj with i, j adjacent.
Remark 2.2. The downward propagation rules in Equation (11) above are referred to as tropical
Lusztig rules, as they are obtained by tropicalizing the relations
xi(t1)xi(t2) = xi(t1 + t2),

root αi. Here the indices i, j are adjacent, and i, k non-adjacent, in the Dynkin diagram.
Finally, of key importance are the intersection theory of weave cycles and their associated variables,
especially for the Lusztig cycles in Definition 2.1. The precise formulas for their intersections are
detailed in [CGG+25b, Sections 4.5 & 4.6], and the variables are described in [CGG+25b, Section 5.2],
cf. also [CW24, Section 4]. In particular, the collection of Lusztig cycles determines an intersection
quiver, as illustrated in Figure 1.
2.2
Ingredients on perverse schobers
The weave schober Fw associated to a Demazure weave w, introduced in Section 3, will be a perverse
schober parametrized by a ribbon graph G. Let us recall the key definitions and properties of such
perverse schobers. We refer to [KS14], [Chr22a, Sections 3.1 & 3.2], or [CHQ23, Section 3] for further
details.
2.2.1
G-parametrized perverse schobers
Let us describe G-parametrized perverse schobers, and the ingredients needed to define them.
Definition 2.3. Let G be a ribbon graph. By definition, its exit path category Exit(G) ∈Cat∞is
the nerve of the 1-category whose objects are the vertices and edges of G, and whose non-identity
morphisms are of the form v →e, where e is an edge of G incident to a vertex v.
By the exodromy equivalence, cf. e.g. [PT22, Theorem 5.17], constructible sheaves on G (under-
stood as a stratified space) valued in the ∞-category of small stable ∞-categories St can equiva-
lently be described as functors Exit(G) →St. Thus we can use Exit(G) in Definition 2.3 and have
Fun(Exit(G), St) as a model for constructible sheaves on G with values in the ∞-category of stable
∞-categories. The notion of perversity in classical sheaf theory, cf. [BBD82], has the following incar-
nation in the context of such sheaves of categories on ribbon graphs.
16


## Page 17


For each n ∈N, let Gn be the ribbon graph with a single vertex v and n incident external edges
e1, . . . , en. The ribbon graph Gn is said to be the n-spider Gn. A perverse schober on Gn is defined
as follows:
Definition 2.4. A perverse schober on the n-spider Gn is a functor F: Exit(Gn) →St such that:
(1) If n = 1, the functor
F : V −→N ,
where V := F(v),
N := F(e),
F := F(v →e),
given by restricting to the unique edge e is required to be spherical.2
(2) If n ≥2, the collection of functors
(Fi : Vn −→Ni)i∈Z/nZ,
where Vn := F(v),
Ni := F(ei),
Fi := F(v →ei),
satisfies that
(a) there exist adjunctions Fi ⊣Gi ⊣Hi for some Gi, Hi,
(b) Gi is fully faithful, which is equivalent to FiGi ≃idNi via the counit,
(c) Fi ◦Gi−1 is an equivalence of ∞-categories,3
(d) Fi ◦Gj ≃0 if j ̸= i, i −1,
(e) fib(Hi−1) = fib(Fi) as full subcategories of Vn.
In Definition 2.4.(1), the functor F uniquely determines F as v, e are the only objects and v →e is
the only 1-morphism in the 1-category defining Exit(Gn). Similarly, in Definition 2.4.(2), the functors
(F1, . . . , Fn) uniquely determine F for n ≥2. Definition 2.4 provides local perversity conditions at Gn.
Passing to Grothendieck groups, these conditions imply that the arising diagram encodes the sections
of a perverse sheaf on the disc with support on Gn, see [KS16]. This notion is globalized to a ribbon
graph as follows:
Definition 2.5 (Perverse schober on G). Let G be a ribbon graph. By definition, G-parametrized
perverse schober is a functor
F: Exit(G) →St
such that, for each n-valent vertex v of G, the restriction of F to the coslice category Exit(G)v/
determines a perverse schober on the n-spider, as in Definition 2.4.
We will mostly be interested in k-linear perverse schobers, meaning that the functor F: Exit(G) →
St factors through LinCatsm
k
→St.
The ∞-category of global sections of a G-parametrized perverse schober is defined as follows:
Definition 2.6. Let F be a G-parametrized perverse schober.
(i) By definition, the ∞-category of global sections of F is
RΓ(G, F) := lim(F) ∈St .
Note that RΓ(G, F) can be identified with the ∞-category of coCartesian sections of the Grothendieck
construction p: Γ(F) →Exit(G) of F, see [Lur24, Prop. 05RX]. Since F arises from a strictly
commuting diagram Exit(G) →Set∆, there is an explicit simplicial set version of the Grothendieck
construction [Lur24, Def. 025X].

Previous papers of the second author used different conventions for the twist and cotwists functors. The convention
employed here is the standard in the literature on spherical objects.
It is employed to simplify the notation in the
computations involving spherical objects.

limit of F in the (∞, 2)-category of stable ∞-categories.
In Definition 2.6, RΓ(G, F) ⊂L(G, F) is a full subcategory.
The focus in this manuscript is on
RΓ(G, F), but L(G, F) is used for gluing constructions of global sections, see for instance Section 7.1.1.

Given a ribbon graph G together with a chosen collection of internal edges, we can contract these
internal edges to produce a new ribbon graph G′. This is denoted by c: G →G′, indicating that G′
arises via contraction from G. In order to understand the behavior of weave schobers under weave
equivalences and weave mutations, we need the following result on how G-parametrized perverse
schober behave under such contractions:
Proposition 2.8 ([Chr22a, Prop. 4.28] & [CHQ23, Lemma 3.16]). Let c: G →G′ be a contraction
of ribbon graphs.
(1) Let F be a G-parametrized perverse schober and assume that c does not contract two singularities
of F into a single vertex. Then there exists a canonical G′-parametrized perverse schober c∗(F)
together with a canonical equivalence of ∞-categories
RΓ(G, F) ≃RΓ(G′, c∗(F)) .
(2) Let F′ be a G′-parametrized perverse schober and choose for every singularity v ∈G′
0 of F a
preimage under c in G0. Then there exists a canonical G-parametrized perverse schober c∗(F)
together with a canonical equivalence of perverse schobers c∗c∗(F′) ≃F′.
Proposition 2.8 will be used shortly when stating and proving Lemma 2.12 below.

3
The perverse schober of a Demazure weave
This section introduces the perverse schober Fw associated to a Demazure weave w in Section 3.1,
and establishes the behavior of its global sections under weave equivalence and weave mutation, in
Section 3.2.
Figure 10: A weave w, depicted left, and its associated graph Gw, drawn to its right. The
trivalent vertices p1, . . . , pm of w and the associated vertices v1, . . . , vm are also depicted,
with vi at the same height as pi, i ∈[1, m]. The edges e1, . . . , em of Gw are also drawn,
with ei denoting the edge between vi−1 and vi. The weave schober Fw is a perverse
schober on Gw.
22


## Page 23


3.1
Definition of weave schobers
Let w : β −→δ(β) be an n-stranded Demazure weave. The height function endows the trivalent
vertices of w with a total order, increasing as we read bottom-to-top. We assume that w is generic,
in that no two vertices of w are at the same height. For instance, if we consider w as a raked weave
as in [CGG+25b, Section 5.1.1], the horizontal right semirays {rv}v∈V (w) do not intersect each other.
These semirays rv are depicted in an example as the dashed yellow segments in Figure 12. As before,
we let p1, . . . , pm be the trivalent vertices of w, ordered from bottom-to-top by height, cf. e.g. Fig-
ure 10. Let us now start describing the perverse schober Fw associated to w.
Domain of definition. Let Gw be the linear spanning graph of the 1-gon with m vertices, as depicted
in Figure 10. In practice, we draw the graph Gw such that:
(i) Gw is drawn vertically to the right side of w,
(ii) The m vertices of Gw are labeled by v1, . . . , vm from bottom-to-top, and the edges by e1, . . . , em,
with e1 being the bottom external (non-compact) edge.
(iii) The ith vertex vi of Gw is drawn at the same height as the trivalent vertex pi ∈w, so that vm
lies on top, v1 on the bottom, and the external edge lies below v1.
See Figures 11 and 12 for illustrations of such Gw. We also consider a generic point v−∞in the
non-compact edge e1. By construction, the perverse schober Fw associated to w will be a perverse
schober parametrized by Gw.
Generic stalks. We choose the generic stalks of the perverse schober Fw, i.e. the stalks at the points
of any edge, to be the perfect derived ∞-category D := Dperf(Π2(An)) of the (additive) derived pre-
projective algebra Π2(An) of type An, also known as the 2-Calabi–Yau completion of type An [Kel11].
This choice is in part motivated by the equivalence between D and the wrapped Fukaya category
of the 4-dimensional An-Milnor fibre, cf. e.g. [LU21, Section 3].4 We denote by S1, . . . , Sn ∈D the
1-dimensional modules associated with the n vertices of the An-quiver, i.e. the simple modules of the
vertices in the standard heart of D. We note that Si ∈D is 2-spherical for all 1 ≤i ≤n, that
Ext1(Si, Sj) ≃k if |i −j| = 1, and Ext1(Si, Sj) ≃0 if |i −j| ̸= 1.
Spherical functors. The spherical functors describing Fw will be defined by spherical objects, which
are themselves constructed as follows. Let p ∈w be a point on the Demazure weave, consider the
horizontal right semiray rp based at p, and suppose that rp does not intersect any vertex of w. The
intersections of rp with w determine a positive braid word βp = si0si1 . . . sil(p) by reading the si-labels
in the associated weave edges left-to-right, cf. Figure 11. In these hypotheses and notation, we define:
Definition 3.1 (Spherical objects from weave points). The spherical object associated to p ∈w is
Sp := Sβp = T −1
Sil(p) · · · T −1
Si2T −1

of Si0 along the right thimble ℘r(p), see Section 7.2 below. More generally, Definition 3.1 should
be understood as the transport of Si0 along the horizontal right semiray rp. In Definition 3.1, the

Proof. For each 2-spherical object S ∈D, by [Chr26b, Lem. 6.4], the functor MorD(S, -): D →Dperf(k)
admits a weak relative left 3-Calabi–Yau structure, and the functor (-) ⊗S : Dperf(k) →D admits

and [Chr26b, Thm. 5.7].
Remark 3.7 (On generalizing to finite Dynkin type). The calculus for Demazure weaves is defined
for any finite Dynkin type g, cf. [CGG+25b, Sections 4 & 6].
Specifically, the simply-laced cases
are constructed directly, and the non-simply-laced cases via folding, cf. [CGG+25b, Section 6.2]. In
line with this, the weave schober Fw in Definition 3.3 can be constructed analogously in any of the
simply-laced cases, where the stalk category D is taken to be the derived ∞-category of the derived
preprojective algebra of the corresponding type g.5
The folding technique applies to extend the
construction of the weave schober in the non-simply-laced cases. In this more general framework, it is
our conceptual understanding that the main results in this manuscript generalize to any finite Dynkin
type in characteristic 0. A number of technical details would need to be written and verified, and thus
we leave the development of categorical weave calculus for any finite Dynkin type as a conjectural
generalization.
3.2
The schober Fw under weave equivalences and mutations
Consider a weave equivalence w ≃w′ between Demazure weaves w, w′, cf. Section 2.1.1 or [CGG+25b,
Section 4.2]. By definition, such an equivalence is said to be height-preserving if the height-induced
order of the trivalent vertices of w coincides with that of the trivalent vertices of w′. Any weave equiva-
lence can be expressed as a (possibly alternating) composition of height-preserving weave equivalences
and the local weave equivalence depicted in Figure 14. We refer to these particular weave equivalences
as pulley moves: specifically, a forward pulley move exchanges Figure 14 (left) for Figure 14 (right),

The distinction between height-preserving weave equivalences and pulley moves is crucial in the study
of weave schobers. Indeed, the exchange of heights for trivalent vertices of a weave, whether caused
by a pulley move or a weave mutation, is what modifies the weave schober in an interesting manner.

permutations si, sj ∈W are also arbitrary.

the two involved trivalent vertices are exchanged. It thus follows that the spherical objects associated
to any other trivalent vertices (other than these two) remain identical. By direct computation, the
effect of such height exchange on the two spherical objects associated to these two trivalent vertices
being exchanged yields Equation (22).

Categorical Lusztig cycles
The object of this section is to construct the categorical Lusztig cycles associated to a weave w, which
will be global sections of the weave schober Fw, i.e. objects in RΓ(Gw, Fw). Their construction requires
as input a number of new results that will be proven in Section 6. Assuming these results for now, the
construction is presented in Section 4.1, while Section 4.2 collects the main statements about them,
which are proven in Section 8. We however first summarize the construction of the categorical Lusztig
cycles as follows:
Outline of the construction. Let w be a Demazure weave and {γw
i } its collection of Lusztig
cycles, i ∈[1, m]. For a fixed Lusztig cycle γw
i , any horizontal slice h ⊆D that does not intersect
a weave vertex specifies a weighted braid word (βh, ah). In a nutshell, the categorical Lusztig cycle
Lw
i is constructed by associating to each slice h a local object Lβh,ah with a filtration adapted to the
weighted braid word (βh, ah), and assembling such Lβh,ah together as the horizontal slice h scans the
weave from the vertex vi downwards. Specifically, these two steps are as follows:
Step 1. Given a weighted braid word (β, a) arising as a slice of a Lusztig cycle, we will show:
∃! rigid filtered object Lβ,a ∈D whose graded pieces recover (β, a)

4.1
Definition of the categorical Lusztig cycles
Let w be a Demazure weave with m trivalent vertices p1, . . . , pm, ordered from bottom to top. The
vertices v1, . . . , vm and edges e1, . . . , em of the graph Gw are also ordered bottom to top, cf. Figure 10.
Given the weave schober Fw, we describe a global section s ∈RΓ(Gw, Fw) by specifying:
1. Its value s(vj) at the vertices vj, i.e. an object in Fw(vj) ≃V×Fj D ≃Dperf(k)×Fj D, if i < m, or
in Dperf(k), if i = m, according to Section 2.2.2 and Section 3, where Fj := -⊗Spj : Dperf(k) →D.
2. Its value s(ej) at the edges ej, i.e. an object in Fw(ej) ≃D.
3. Its value s(vj →ej) on the generization morphisms vj →ej, which is an object in Fw(vj →ej),
i.e. a functor Fw(vj) →Fw(ej), and similarly for vj →ej+1, j ∈[1, m −1], and vm →em.
These values for s need to be compatible with each other and Fw in order for them to define an actual
global section in RΓ(Gw, Fw), e.g. so it is coCartesian and not just lax, cf. Definition 2.6.

4.2
The main results on categorical Lusztig cycles
Let us collect the main results that we will establish on the categorical Lusztig cycles from Defi-
nition 4.1. To ease notation, given a Demazure weave w with m trivalent vertices, we write {Lw
i }
implicitly understanding that the index always ranges in i ∈[1, m].
(1) First, we show that the collection of categorical Lusztig cycles is invariant under weave equiv-
alence. Specifically, let w, w′ be two Demazure weaves and Fw, Fw′ their associated weave schobers.
Recall that given a weave equivalence w ≃w′, Theorem 3.8.(3) provides an equivalence of ∞-categories
between the global sections Fw and Fw′, cf. Equation (23). The precise invariance reads as follows:
Theorem 4.5 (Categorical Lusztig cycles are invariant under weave equivalence). Let w, w′ be two
Demazure weaves, w ≃w′ a weave equivalence and ε : RΓ(Gw, Fw)

In a nutshell, Theorem 4.5 states that weave equivalences yield the same collections of categorical
Lusztig cycles, up to reordering. Conceptually, it makes the collection of categorical Lusztig cycles
well-defined as a collection of objects assigned to a weave equivalence class, in the same manner that
the isomorphism type of the ∞-category RΓ(Gw, Fw) is well-defined on the equivalence class of w,
even if the weave schober Fw itself is not. Theorem 4.5 is proven in Section 8.2.

a weave w forms a simple-minded collection. Easing the notation by Hw := RΓ(Gw, Fw), this means
that each categorical Lusztig cycle Lw

Theorem 4.6 (Categorical Lusztig cycles are simple-minded). Let w be a Demazure weave. Then the

such that the simple objects in its heart are given by the categorical Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }.

5
Filtered objects from weighted braid words
The goal of this section is to construct filtered objects Lβ,a ∈D from weighted braid words (β, a).
The geometric realizations of these filtered objects are key for the definition of the categorical Lusztig
cycles in Definition 4.1, see e.g. Statement (27). That said, the theory we develop in this section is
more general and thus we present it in a self-contained manner, entirely within the ∞-category D,
and detached from the theory of weaves and their Lusztig cycles. We shall only specialize to weighted
braid words (β, a) arising from Lusztig cycles in Section 6, where Statement (27), in the more precise
form of Corollary 6.8, is proven using the general techniques developed in the present section.
The input ingredients for this section are:
1. The perfect derived ∞-category D := Dperf(Π2(An)) of the additive derived preprojective algebra
Π2(An) of type An. Its simples are denoted by S1, . . . , Sn ∈D, as in Section 3.1.
2. Given a positive braid word β := si0si1 . . . sil, we define its associated spherical object Sβ to be
Sβ := T −1
Sil · · · T −1
Si2T −1
Si1(Si0) ∈D
(31)
where TSj = cofib(MorD(Sj, -) ⊗Sj →idD) is the twist functor along the 2-spherical object Sj.
This is in line with Definition 3.1.
The section is organized as follows:
(a) Section 5.1 establishes homological bounds on MorD(Sα, Sβ) for certain types of positive braid
words α, β, and shows that the 2-spherical semi-twists preserve rigidity. Both these technical
ingredients are crucial for the existence and uniqueness of the filtered objects Lβ,a ∈D associated
to weighted braid words (β, a).
(b) Section 5.2 provides a diagrammatic model, based on the study of matching paths, to describe
a subcategory M ⊆D. Lemma 5.25 will establish that M is stable under a class of semi-twists.
These results in Section 5.2 and the subcategory M are used to prove the existence of the filtered
objects Lβ,a ∈D associated to certain weighted braid words (β, a).
(c) Section 5.3 proves the uniqueness of the rigid filtered objects Lβ,a in D, should they exist, and
establishes a criterion, based on the diagrammatic model of Section 5.2, for the existence of such
Lβ,a given a weighted braid word (β, a).
The main result that we prove in this section, and use subsequently, is the following:
Theorem 5.1 (Uniqueness of filtered objects Lβ,a). Let (β, a) be a weighted braid word. Suppose that
there exists a (β, a)-filtered object Lβ,a : N(Z≥0) →D whose geometric realization is rigid and lies in
M. Then any other rigid (β, a)-filtered object in D is equivalent to Lβ,a.
The necessary definitions for all the notions featured in Theorem 5.1, including that of a (β, a)-filtered
object, the subwords βi, βj, the degree 1 crossings, and the subcategory M ⊆D, will be provided in
Section 5.2 and Section 5.3 below. Theorem 5.1 is proven in Section 5.3.

5.1
Bounds on Ext-groups between braid 2-spherical objects Sβ
This subsection proves Lemma 5.4 and Lemma 5.6, establishing the vanishing of certain Ext-groups
between certain objects of D.
First, the silting object Π2(An) ∈D determines a t-structure, cf. Lemma A.19, which we refer to
as the standard t-structure. The objects Sβ typically do not belong to its associated heart D♡⊆D.
For instance, if β = sk
1 then Ssk
1 = S1[k −1] ̸∈D♡for any k ∈[2, ∞). That said, it is well known that
β reduced =⇒Sβ ∈D♡.
(32)
Remark 5.2. (1) Suppose that β = sil . . . si0 is reduced. Since the Grothendieck group K0(D♡) is
freely generated by the n simples S1, . . . , Sn, it canonically identifies with the root lattice in type
An. Then the module Sβ ∈D♡projects to the positive root sil . . . si1(αi0) in K0(D♡).
(2) By [HW22, Lemma 3.3], if an object lies in the homologically non-positive aisle of the standard
t-structure of D, then so do its images under the action of the negative spherical twists. Therefore
the spherical object Sβ ∈D lies in the homologically negative aisle of the t-structure of D.
Remark 5.3. For a reduced braid word β, the repeated (positive) spherical twists {Sα}α<β ⊂D♡
are also known as the layer modules, and arise as the graded pieces of a filtration of the corresponding
preprojective algebra, see [AIRT12, Prop. 2.2] and [Can25, Rem. 4.4]. We also note that negative
spherical twists correspond to positive spherical twists in the opposite category, and that since D ≃
Dop, the usage of negative or positive spherical twists can be considered a convention.
Given two positive braid words α, β, let us denote by α < β if α is a strict suffix of β, i.e. if there
exist i0, . . . , im such that β = si0si1 . . . simα and α ̸= β. Note that for reduced permutation braids,
the corresponding partial order is the Bruhat order. The following vanishing result for the morphism
objects (i.e. derived Homs) between such two 2-spherical objects will be crucial.

heart D♡and a simple Sil. Thus Exti

Let us now prove the general non-reduced case by an induction on the length l.

5.2
A geometric model for D
In this subsection we explain how to describe certain objects in D and the morphisms between them
in terms of immersed paths in the plane. Such objects and their geometric description will be used
in Section 5.3 to construct the filtered objects Lβ,a. In brief, this subsection develops the classical
theory of matching cycles in the context of a perverse schober whose ∞-category of global sections is
equivalent to D.
Figure 17: The graph GD in the case of n = 3, with its four vertices v1, v2, v3, v4 (gray) and
its three edges e1, e2, e3 (black). There are three πD-matching paths γ1, γ2, γ3, depicted
in yellow, purple and gray respectively.
Let GD be the linear ribbon graph with n+1 vertices, i.e. an An+1-Dynkin diagram. See Figure 17
for the case n = 3. Consider the spherical functor ϕ: Dperf(k) −→Dperf(k[t±1]), with k[t±1] the
ungraded k-algebra of Laurent polynomials, determined by mapping k to the simple representation
with k as its underlying vector space and t acting by 1. Given such a functor ϕ we define, using
Notation 2.10, the following k-linear GD-parametrized perverse schober:
FD :=

It follows from [Chr21, Thm. 4.19] that there exists an equivalence of k-linear ∞-categories D ≃
RΓ(GD, FD), i.e. D is equivalent to the category of global sections of the schober FD in (45).
Remark 5.8. Specifically, this partial geometric model that we will describe captures, in the context
of perverse schobers, aspects of [Sei08, Section (16g)], and some of the algebraic structures associated
to the symplectic geometry of Lagrangian 2-spheres. See also [KS02, Sections 3&4], [Chr21, Section
5] and [CHQ23, Section 4].
5.2.1
Matching objects in D
Let D ⊂R2 the open disk with center (n+3
2 , 0) and diameter n+2. Consider the points vi = (i, 0) ∈D,
i ∈[1, n+1], and the punctured disk ˜D := D\{v1, . . . , vn+1}. By definition, a grading structure ν on D
is a homotopy class of sections of the projectivised tangent bundle P(T ˜D); a section of P(T ˜D) is also
known as a line field. We equip the disk D above with the unique grading structure satisfying that the
winding number around each puncture is given by 2. The corresponding line field ν can then be cho-
sen to be constant, pointing everywhere in the horizontal direction. In particular, ν extends to all of D.
Let us fix an embedding of the geometric realization of GD into D such that the i-th vertex vi of
GD is mapped to vi ∈D. To ease notation, we do not distinguish the vertices of GD from their images
in D and equally refer to them as vi. Note that in this model we are depicting GD and the schober
FD horizontally, cf. Figure 17 and Equation (45): this is done intentionally in order to contrast with
Gw and Fw, which we always draw vertically, cf. Section 3.1.
Definition 5.9. By definition, an immersed curve γ : [−1, 1] −→D is a πD-matching path if
39


## Page 40


• The image γ((−1, 1)) is disjoint from the vertices of GD, and its endpoints γ(−1) and γ(1) must
be vertices of GD.
• The complement D \ γ(−1, 1) does not contain any once-punctured disks without marked points
on the boundary, and whose boundary entirely consists of points in the image of γ.8
• γ is not homotopic relative the vertices of GD to a constant curve.
The πD-matching paths in Definition 5.9 will be always considered up to homotopy relative to the
vertices of GD. Figure 17 depicts three embedded πD-matchings paths in the case n = 3.
Definition 5.10. A grading of a πD-matching path γ : [−1, 1] →D consists of a homotopy class of
paths in Γ([−1, 1], γ∗P(T ˜D)) from the line field γ∗ν to the line field of tangents ˙γ.
Remark 5.11. Gradings of a πD-matching path γ, as in Definition 5.10, form a Z-torsor. We can and
typically do choose the line field ν that is parallel to the edges of GD. Note that choosing γ(0) to lie
on an edge e and ˙γ(0) to be parallel to e determines a grading, i.e. a base point for the Z-torsor.
Figure 18: The six different types of local pieces for a πD-matching path, as defined in
Definition 5.9.
Each πD-matching path from Definition 5.9 can be uniquely written as the composite of six different
homotopy classes of segments, up to reversal of orientation and smooth isotopy relative to its endpoints,
with each segment lying in a contractible neighborhood of a vertex vi ∈GD. These six types of
segments are depicted in Figure 18, paired in three different columns. The segments depicted in the
kth column of Figure 18 are said to be segments of the kth type. For instance, the three πD-matchings
paths depicted in Figure 17 only use segments of the first and second type.
In our context, the purpose of the πD-matching paths from Definition 5.9 is that we can associate a
global section Xγ of FD, i.e. an object Xγ ∈D, to each such graded πD-matching path γ. This can
be seen as an instance of the constructions given in [Chr21, Constr. 5.13] and [CHQ23, Section 4.3].9
The construction can be described as follows.
Construction 5.12 (Matching objects). Let us label the vertices of GD by v1, . . . , vn+1 left-to-right
and the incident edges by e1, . . . , en, also left-to-right. Fix a πD-matching path γ: the object Xγ ∈D
will be obtained by gluing local sections associated to the different types of segments for γ, as in
Figure 18.
(5.12.1). First, let us choose the grading ν for γ as in Remark 5.11: such grading of γ deter-
mines an integer d. From this, we associate a degree d(δ) ∈Z≤0 to every type of segment δ for γ,

Definition 5.14 (Matching objects). Let γ be a graded πD-matching path. By definition, the object

matching if X ∈M.

Remark 5.17. Let γ be a πD-matching path without segments of the third type. Then there exists
a canonical grading for γ such that Xγ ∈D♡lies in the standard heart D♡⊂D.
Applying a spherical twist to a matching object Xγ ∈M along any of the simples S1, . . . , Sn can be

5.2.2
Morphisms between matching objects in D
Let us now provide a description of the morphism objects between the matching objects from Sec-
tion 5.2.1 in terms of counts of graded intersections between their corresponding πD-matching paths.
43


## Page 44


We distinguish two types of intersections: endpoint intersections and crossings. Crossings are trans-
verse intersections of two πD-matching paths away from their endpoints. Both types of intersections
are considered as directed: the order of the curves at the intersection point is specified.
Index of intersection points.
First, we must associate an index d(x) ∈Z to each type of
directed intersection x, as follows. For the case of crossings, let γ1, γ2 be two πD-matching paths
and ci :=

Suppose that γ1, γ2 intersect trans-
versely at an interior point p = γ1(t1) = γ2(t2), for some t1, t2 ∈(−1, 1).
Then we define the
index d(x) ∈π1(P(TpS)) ≃Z to be the class c2(t2)·κ·c1(t1)−1, where κ is a path from ˙γ1(t1) to ˙γ2(t2)
given by clockwise rotation in TpD by an angle less than π.10 In this case, the crossing is said to be in
degree d(x), d(x)+1, since these are the degrees of the non-trivial extensions to which it will contribute.
For the case of endpoint intersections, let γ1, γ2 be two πD-matching paths with an endpoint intersec-
tion x = vi for some i ∈[1, n + 1]. We follow [IQZ20, Eq. (2.5)] to define the intersection index of x.
For that, we choose a small circle around vi and let α: [0, 1] →D be a curve going fully around the
circle in the clockwise direction. Let y1 be the crossing from γ1 to α, and y2 the crossing from γ2 to
α, so that y1, y2 are crossing intersections. The index of the intersection point x is then defined as
d(x) := d(y1) −d(y2), where we have chosen an arbitrary grading for the circle α.

(2) For crossings with immersed πD-matching paths arising from self-extensions as in Remark 5.20,
the fibers of both morphisms arising from the crossing can be matching (and be computed by choosing
a different representative). However, if the fiber of a morphism between two matching objects arising
from an intersection is not matching, it must arise from a crossings and lie in the larger of the two
degrees of the crossing.

5.3
The filtered object Lβ,a associated with a weighted braid word (β, a)
The object of this subsection is to prove Theorem 5.1. Let us formally define the notions featured in
its statement, including that of a weighted braid word (β, a) and a (β, a)-filtration.
46


## Page 47


Definition 5.26 (Weighted braid words). Let β = σi0 . . . σil be a positive braid word of length l + 1.
By definition, a weight a = (a0, . . . , al) for β consists of a sequence of non-negative integers ai ≥0. A
weighted braid word is a pair (β, a) consisting of a positive braid word β and a weight for β.
In the case of permutations, Definition 5.26 appeared in [CGG+25a, Definition 6.13] in the form of
weighted expressions and their associated Lusztig data. Recall from [Lur17, Definition 1.2.2.9] that a
Z≥0-filtered object in D is a functor N(Z≥0) →D, where N(Z≥0) is the nerve of Z≥0, with Z≥0 being
understood as a poset category. Given a positive braid word β = σi0 . . . σil we denote
βj := σijσij+1 . . . σil,
j ∈[0, l]
(48)
Note that β0 = β and βl = σil.
Definition 5.27 ((β, a)-filtered objects). Let (β, a) be a weighted braid word of length l + 1 and
X ∈D an object. By definition, a (β, a)-filtration of X is a Z≥0-filtered object X(-): N(Z≥0) →D
such that:
1. the ith graded piece gri(X(-)) := cof(X(i −1) →X(i)) is
gri(X(-)) ≃

2. As objects in D, there exists an equivalence colim X(-) ≃X.
By definition, a (β, a)-filtered object X ∈D is an object X ∈D equipped with a (β, a)-filtration.
Remark 5.28. Given a Z≥0-filtered object X(-) in D, the colimit colim X(-) is also called the geo-
metric realization of X(-), as it corresponds to the (simplicial) geometric realization under the Lurie–
Dold–Kan correspondence, cf. [Lur17, Rem. 1.2.4.2]. In the setting of Definition 5.27, colim X(-) is
equivalent to X(l).
In general, there might be multiple (β, a)-filtered objects in D. To obtain uniqueness, as in Theo-
rem 5.1, we focus on rigid objects. Recall that an object X ∈D is called rigid if Ext1
D(X, X) ≃0.

5.3.2
Resolving endpoint intersections
The remaining ingredient before proving Theorem 5.1 is used to construct the rigid filtered objects Lβ,a:
it is the construction of certain morphisms whose cofibers are rigid, as established in Proposition 5.36
below. For that, we start with the cyclic order on endpoint intersections at a vertex of GD, defined
as follows:
Definition 5.34 (Cyclic order at vertices). Let v ∈GD be a vertex and C := {γ1, . . . , γm} be a
collection of distinct πD-matching paths all of which have an endpoint at v ∈GD, arriving at v
with different slopes. By definition, the intersection order on C is the cyclic order on C obtained by
choosing representatives of the πD-matching paths in C which have minimal numbers of intersections
with each other, and declaring the cyclic order to be induced by the clockwise orientation. That is, in
the intersection order, γi < γj < γk if and only if the angle with which γj approaches v lies between
the angles with which γk and γi approach v.
In Definition 5.34, angles are understood to lie in [0, 2π), and cf. [KS02, Section 3a] for a discussion
on representatives of paths with minimal number of intersections.
Remark 5.35. An equivalent and more algebraic version of Definition 5.34 is that γi < γj < γk if
and only if the composite of the non-zero morphisms Xγi →Xγj[a] and the [a] shift of Xγj →Xγk[b]
arising from the endpoint intersections of γi and γj, and γj and γk, yields a non-zero morphism
Xγi →Xγk[a + b].
Proposition 5.36. Let X ∈M be rigid object and S ∈M a 2-spherical object.
Suppose that
Exti
D(S, X) ≃0, i ∈[2, ∞), and that there are no degree 1 crossings from S to X.
Then there
exists a unique morphism f : S[−1] →X, unique up to postcomposition with automorphisms of X,
whose cofiber cof(f) is rigid. Furthermore, this cofiber is matching, i.e. cof(f) ∈M.
Proof. Let us write Y := cof(f), and express S = Xγ for some πD-matching path γ. Let v, v′ ∈GD
be the two vertices of where γ ends. Consider the induced intersection order, cf. Definition 5.34, on
the set C, resp. C′, consisting of those πD-matching paths describing X ∈M with an endpoint at
v, resp. v′, and γ itself. We turn this cyclic orders on C and C′ into total orders by declaring γ
to be the largest element in this total order. In each of these two total orders, consider the small-
est element such that the corresponding endpoint intersection with γ is of degree 1. These yield two
morphisms a, a′ : S[−1] →X, one from such smallest element in C and the corresponding one from C′.
Now, we claim that the required morphism is their sum f := a + a′, f : S[−1] →X. Let us argue
that cof(f) must be rigid. First, note that the cofiber of any morphism S[−1] →X not containing
contributions from both a and a′ cannot itself be rigid. Indeed, passing to the cofiber amounts to
resolving the endpoint intersections, cf. Figure 20, and resolving degree 1 endpoint intersections dif-
ferent than the minimal degree 1-ones leads to a self-crossing of the cofiber near v, or v′, of degrees 0
and 1, which contradicts rigidity. Now, to show that cof(f) is rigid, note that if a or a′ corresponds
to an endpoint intersection with non-minimal elements (though minimal of degree 1), the assumption
Extj
D(S, X) ≃0, j ∈[2, ∞), and the rigidity Ext1(X, X) ≃0 imply that either a or a′ must factor as
S[−1] →X[−i] →X for some i ∈(−∞, −2]. That said, the degree (−i + 1)-endpoint intersection
leads to self-crossings of cof(a + a′) in degrees (−i + 1), (−i + 2) and i, (i + 1), and since these are not
degree 1, we conclude cof(f) is rigid.
Finally, for uniqueness, note that the degree 0 endpoint intersections of X with itself at v and
v′ give rise to automorphisms of X which allow to remove all contributions from degree 1 endpoint
intersections that are larger in the total order, as follows from Remark 5.35. Thus the morphism is
uniquely determined up to such automorphisms, as required.

5.3.3
Proof of Theorem 5.1
Proof of Theorem 5.1. Let us suppose that the (β, a)-filtered object Lβ,a exists and prove its unique-
ness, up to equivalence. Let L′
β,a be another (β, a)-filtered object with rigid geometric realization. We
now prove that the restrictions of Lβ,a and L′
β,a to N(Z[0,i]) are equivalent, by induction on i ∈[0, ∞).
The base case i = 0 holds by construction. For the inductive step, we suppose that the assertion holds
for i −1, and show it for i, as follows. By definition, the ith object Lβ,a(i) of the filtered object Lβ,a
arises as the cofiber of a morphism S⊕ai
βi [−1] →Lβ,a(i −1) or, equivalently, as the ai-many iterated
cofibers along morphisms with domain Sβi[−1]. By Corollary 5.33, Lβ,a(i) is rigid and lies in M, and
thus Sβi and Lβ,a(i−1) ≃L′
β,a(i−1) cannot have a crossing contributing to degrees 0 and 1. Proposi-
tion 5.36 thus shows that there exists a unique morphism f : S⊕ai
βi [−1] →Lβ,a(i−1), up to equivalence,
whose cofiber is rigid. Therefore, the morphisms Lβ,a(i −1) →Lβ,a(i) and Lβ,a(i −1)′ →Lβ,a(i)′
must both be equivalent to the cofiber morphism Lβ,a(i −1) →cof(f) of f. We thus obtain that
the restrictions of Lβ,a and L′
β,a to N(Z[0,i]) are also equivalent, concluding the induction step. This
establishes the uniqueness of Lβ,a, up to equivalence.
For context, thought it will not be used in this article, we record our expectation that the following
variation of Theorem 5.1 might hold:
50


## Page 51


Conjecture 5.38. Let (β, a) be a weighted braid word. Then there exists a unique (β, a)-filtered object
Lβ,a ∈D whose geometric realization is rigid.
Conjecture 5.38 makes no assertion that Lβ,a ∈M, cf. Theorem 5.1. An approach to such conjecture
via our argument for Theorem 5.1 would require understanding how to generalize the endpoint reso-
lutions, cf. the proof of Proposition 5.36, to interact with degree 1 crossings while preserving rigidity,
cf. Example 5.37.

can be alternatively described in terms of a coherent chain complex, as follows from the ∞-categorical
Dold–Kan correspondence, see also Remark B.3. Appendix B provides a summary on coherent chain
complexes. Intuitively, the role of coherent chain complexes in a stable ∞-category is akin to that
of twisted complexes of A∞-modules in a A∞-category. In a nutshell, the graded pieces of any Z≥0-
filtered object X(-) in D determine a D-valued coherent chain complex. Specifically, the i-th term of
such a coherent chain complex is the shift of the corresponding piece of the associated graded, i.e. the
cofiber cof(fi)[−i], where fi : X(i −1) →X(i) is the morphism defining the filtered object.In our case
this coherent chain complex is described as follows.

Remark 5.40. Even though the coherent chain complexes Cβ,a contains the same data as the filtered
objects Lβ,a, it is sometimes convenient to use them when establishing results related to Lβ,a. In
particular, we use this presentation in terms of coherent chain complexes in the proofs of some of the
results on categorical Lusztig cycles, cf. the proofs of Proposition 6.2 and Proposition 6.5.
(C) The role of rigidity. We conclude this section with an example illustrating the behavior of
non-rigid (β, a)-filtered objects under the tropical propagation rules for weave cycles. These weighted
braid words do arise as slices of weave cycles, though not as slices of Lusztig cycles. We hope this
example highlights the importance of rigidity in our constructions.

6
Weave propagation of rigid filtered objects
The goal of this section is to establish how a rigid (β, a)-filtered object Lβ,a, in the sense of Defi-
nition 5.27, changes when either a weighted braid move, or a weighted s2
i →si transformation, are
applied to (β, a), cf. Section 2.1.2. Specifically, we prove the equivalence in Statement (30) and prove
the cofiber sequence from Equation (28), in Proposition 6.2 and Proposition 6.5 respectively. The for-
mer implies that the geometric realization of Lβ,a is invariant under a weighted braid move, whereas
the latter exhibits the homological relation between Lβ,a and the filtered object associated to the
weighted braid word obtained from (β, a) via a weighted s2
i →si transformation.
Given a weighted braid word (β, a) such that a rigid (β, a)-filtered object Lβ,a exists, we denote
by
Lβ,a := colim Lβ,a
the geometric realization of Lβ,a, cf. Remark 5.28.
6.1
Propagation via weighted braid moves
The weighted analogue of the braid moves is as follows:
Definition 6.1 (Weighted braid moves). Let (β, a) be a weighted braid word.
By definition, a
weighted braid move (β, a) →(β′, a′) is a braid move β →β′ such that:
1. If β →β′ is of the form sisi+1si ↔si+1sisi+1, applied to the j, (j + 1) and (j + 2) crossings of
β, then the weights satisfy
(a′

j+2) = (aj+1 + aj+2 −min(aj, aj+2), min(aj, aj+2), aj + aj+1 −min(aj, aj+2)) ,
and else a′
k = ak if k ̸∈{j, j + 1, j + 2}.
2. If β →β′ is of the form sisq ↔sqsi, |q −i| ≥2, applied to the j, (j + 1)st crossings of β, then
the weights satisfy
(a′
j, a′
j+1) = (aj+1, aj) ,
and else a′
k = ak if k ̸∈{j, j + 1}.
In short, a weighted braid move is a braid move along with a change of the associated weights according
to the tropical propagation rules in Equation (11).
By the construction of Lusztig cycles, cf. Section 2.1.2, the weighted braid words associated to a Lusztig
cycle before and after a 4-valent or 6-valent vertex of a weave w are related by a single weighted braid
move as in Definition 6.1. Consequently, the move in Definition 6.1.(1) is referred to as a 6-valent
move, or a weighted Reidemeister 3 move, and the move in Definition 6.1.(2) is said to be 4-valent
move, or a weighted commutation move.
Let us now show that, under the assumption of rigidity, being filtered by a weighted braid word
is preserved under weighted braid moves. This provides a categorical interpretation of the tropical
propagation rules for Lusztig cycles, as described in Section 2.1.2.
Proposition 6.2. Let (β, a) be weighted braid word and X ∈D a rigid (β, a)-filtered object. Suppose
that (β, a) →(β′, a′) is a weighted braid move. Then X also admits a (β′, a′)-filtration.

Corollary 6.3 (Invariance of Lβ,a under weighted braid moves). Let w be a Demazure weave and
γi a Lusztig cycle. Suppose that (β, a) and (β′, a′) are two weighted braid words obtained from two
different horizontal slices of γi such that there are no trivalent vertices of w between them. Then there
exists an equivalence Lβ,a ≃Lβ′,a′ in D.
