6.2
Propagation across trivalent vertices
The goal of this subsection is to prove the cofiber sequence (28), which we establish in Proposition 6.5.
Specifically, given a weighted braid word (β, a) with Lβ,a rigid, we want to relate Lβ,a and Lβ′,a′
where (β′, a′) is obtained from (β, a) via the 3-valent move, meaning the weighted braid operation
s2
i →si applied to two consecutive and equal crossings of β, where the weights change according to
56


## Page 57


Equation (11). That is, all weights remain equal except the weight of the new crossing, which must
be the minimum of the two weights associated to the two crossings to which we applied the operation.
For context, we recall from Section 4 that the cofiber sequence (28) is a central ingredient in the
construction of the categorical Lusztig cycles. In particular, by Remark 2.11, it supplies a collection of
relative triangles which are used in Definition 4.1 to specify the required global sections of the weave
schober. The statement we need is as follows:
Proposition 6.5 (Filtered objects across trivalents). Let w be a Demazure weave, p ∈w a trivalent
vertex and γ the Lusztig cycle of a trivalent vertex above p. Consider the weighted braid words (β, a)
and (β′, a′) associated to γ from horizontal slices right above and right below p, respectively, and
suppose that a rigd (β, a)-filtered object Lβ,a exists. Then a rigid (β′, a′)-filtered object Lβ′,a′ exists
and there exists a cofiber sequence in D

Corollary 6.8. Let (β, a) be a weighted braid word arising from a horizontal slice of a Lusztig cycle
of a Demazure weave. Then, up to equivalence, there exists a unique (β, a)-filtered object Lβ,a ∈D
whose geometric realization Lβ,a is rigid. In addition, Lβ,a ∈M.
Proof. Let p ∈w be the vertex associated to the Lusztig cycle γ = γp, so that γ is born at p and
propagates downwards, cf. Definition 2.1. By construction, the weights of the Lusztig cycle above p
vanish, and therefore the statement trivially holds for horizontal slices above p. For the horizontal
slice right below p and the Lusztig cycle γp, the associated weighted braid word (βp, ap) has a unique
non-zero weight aj, for some j ∈[0, ℓ(β)], and this weight has value aj = 1. Thus Lβp,ap ≃S⊕aj
βj
≃Sβj,
which is indeed rigid and belongs to M, since Sβj ∈M by Lemma 5.18. The object Lβp,ap is trivially
(β, a)-filtered and Theorem 5.1 implies it is unique. The statement thus holds for the horizontal slice
59


## Page 60


right below p by taking Lβp,ap with the trivial filtration and colimit Lβp,ap.
Let us now argue that for any horizontal slice h of the Demazure weave w below p. By construction,
the weighted braid word (βh, ah) associated to γp and such horizontal slice h is obtained from (βp, ap) by
a combination of weighted braid moves and (weighted) trivalent moves s2
i →si, typically intertwined.
For the weighted braid moves, Proposition 6.2 and Corollary 6.3 imply the required statement. For
the weighted trivalent moves, i.e. propagating downwards through a 3-valent vertex, Proposition 6.5
shows that the resulting filtered object below exists and it is obtained by applying a positive semi-twist
to the filtered object above, cf. equivalence (74). Lemma 5.6 shows that rigidity is therefore preserved
when scanning downward past a trivalent vertex, and Lemma 5.25 proves that being matching is also
preserved, i.e. the result filtered object has colimit in M. This establishes the existence of the required
(β, a)-filtered rigid object Lβ,a ∈D, with geometric realization in M. Its uniqueness follows from
Theorem 5.1.
This concludes the proofs for all the ingredients needed in the construction of the categorical
Lusztig cycles in Section 4. In particular, the results thus far prove Theorem A. For reference, we
record the proof as follows.
Proof of Theorem A. Part (1) is stated and proven in Corollary 6.8. The proof for Part (2) follows
from combining Corollary 6.3 and Proposition 6.5, cf. also Remark 4.3. Part (3) is obtained from
the construction of the categorical Lusztig cycles in Section 4.1, which is detailed and shown to be
well-defined in the results of Section 5 and this section.
7
Matching sections for weave schobers and weave thimbles
The goal of this section is to construct a full exceptional collection ∆w := {∆1w, . . . , ∆w
m} in the k-
linear stable ∞-category of global sections RΓ(Gw, Fw), with Fw the weave schober of a Demazure
weave w. The exceptional collection ∆w will be used to prove homological properties of the categorical
Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }. In particular, we use them to prove Parts (2), (3) and (4) of Theorem B and
Theorem C.
It is important to emphasize that the exceptional collection ∆w is not invariant under weave equiv-
alences: there exist weave equivalences w ≃w′ such that ∆w ̸= ∆w′. Thus these exceptional collections
∆w are not intrinsically well-defined within the context of weave calculus. That said, the categorial
Lusztig cycles {Lw
i } can be expressed in terms of the collection ∆w in a manner that {Lw
i } are invariant
under weave equivalences applied to w.
This section is organized as follows:
1. Section 7.1 provides a general construction, in line with Section 5.2.1, to obtain global sections
in RΓ(Gw, Fw) from a certain type of matching paths on the plane of the weave w. Note that,
in contrast with Section 5.2, the matching paths in Section 7.1 are drawn on the plane where
the weave w and the graph Gw are themselves drawn, and not in a different plane and with a
different graph GD as it occurred in Section 5.2. The resulting objects in this section belong to
RΓ(Gw, Fw) and are, in a sense, 3-dimensional, cf. Lemma 7.9; the objects built in Section 5.2.1
belonged to D and were, in a sense, 2-dimensional, cf. Section 5.2.2.
2. Section 7.2 provides a specific type of paths associated to a weave and discusses the notion and
properties of weave parallel transport. The key geometric idea is to think of the weave w as a
set of branch cuts. Even if it is a highly non-minimal branch cut, its rigid algebraic structure
leads to remarkable relations between the different objects associated to the different types of
weave paths.
60


## Page 61


3. Section 7.3 uses the general construction from Section 7.1 applied to the weave paths in Sec-
tion 7.2 to define the weave thimbles ∆w of a Demazure weave w, and their duals. A significant
observation will be that these weave thimbles identify with the inductions of the vanishing cycles
in the sense of [Chr25b]. Section 7.3.1 proves that the weave thimbles ∆w form an exceptional
collection, and establishes their behavior under weave equivalences and weave mutations.
7.1
Global sections of Fw from matching paths
Let w be a Demazure weave and p1, . . . , pm be its ordered trivalent vertices, ordered from bottom
to top. Following Section 3.1, we have the associated ribbon graph Gw with vertices v1, . . . , vm and
edges e, . . . , em, see Figure 10. As before, we use v−∞∈e1 to denote a generic point in the unique
non-compact edge e1. To ease notation, we will often denote the trivalent vertices p1, . . . , pm of the
weave w by v1, . . . , vm, identifying pi = vi if the context allows. Note that the graph Gw is always
drawn vertically, in contrast to GD in Section 5.2, which was drawn horizontally, cf. Section 5.2.1.
Both w and Gw are drawn in a plane, equivalently a disk, that we denote by D.
Definition 7.1 (w-matching paths). Let w be a Demazure weave with trivalent vertices p1, . . . , pm.
By definition, a w-matching path γ in D is an embedded curve γ : [0, 1] →D, such that:
(1) γ(0) = vi and γ(1) = vk, for some k ∈{−∞, 1, . . . , m −1} and i ∈(k, m].
(2) Near γ(0) the image of γ must coincide with either Figure 25.(1) or Figure 25.(2). That is, either
point straight to the right, or point immediately to the left after having started with a velocity
vector pointing upwards.
(3) γ goes only downwards after its start, i.e. with the exception of the local model near γ(0) in the
previous item, the path γ goes downwards.
(4) The interior of its image γ(0, 1) is disjoint from {vi}1≤i≤m,
(5) If j ̸= −∞, there must exist an equivalence
Sb ≃F→(γ|(0,1))(Sa)[l]
in D for some l ∈Z, where a, b are the indices such that the weave edges incident to pi are labeled
by a, i.e. by the permutation sa, and the weave edges incident to pk are labeled by b. Here we also
denoted F→(γ|(0,1)): Fw(ei) ≃Fw(ek+1) as the transport of F along γ, cf. [Chr25b, Section 4.3].
Such w-matching paths are considered up to ambient compactly supported smooth isotopy of D relative
to {v−∞, v1, . . . , vn, }.
Note that Definition 7.1 is purposefully ambiguous given the abuse of notation pi = vi identifying
trivalents of w with vertices of Gw. Indeed, it could either suggest that we are drawing a path γ with
endpoints on the trivalent vertices of w, or with endpoints on the vertices of Gw. Both viewpoints
are valuable: the former is more intuitively geometric and matches the classical theory of matching
paths, where the endpoints are branch points, whereas the latter is technically more accurate in the
context of building sections for the weave schober Fw, which is actually defined on Gw. Figure 23 is
an instance of the former viewpoint, drawing endpoints at the trivalents of weave w itself.
Remark 7.2. The condition Definition 7.1.(3) exists to fix the correct shift: e.g. the different angles
in which a path leaves a trivalent of w only affect the resulting object by a shift. Also, in contrast to
Section 5.2.2, we do not need to consider gradings for the w-matching paths in Definition 7.1. The
condition Definition 7.1.(5) is an algebraic version of the requirement that parallel transport along γ
connects the two vanishing spheres at the endpoints of γ, see Section 7.1.2.(iii).

Definition 7.3. Given i ∈[1, m], let Dvi ⊂D be a closed horizontal band whose upper boundary,
resp. lower boundary, coincides with a horizontal slices immediately above, resp. below, the vertex vi.
(1) For i ∈[1, m−1], a w-matching segment in Dvi is defined to be an embedded curve γ : [0, 1] →Dvi
such that
• γ is smoothly isotopic to a path that only goes downwards, relative to its endpoints.
• γ does not intersect {vi} except for at most at one endpoint.
• All endpoints of γ lie in (∂Dvi ∩D◦) ∪{vi}, i.e. on the top or bottom bounding slice or at vi.
Such w-matching segments are considered up to compactly supported smooth isotopy of Dvi
relative Dvi ∪{vi}.
(2) If i = m, a matching segment γ in Dvm is defined similarly, now imposing that γ(0) = vm and
that γ(1) lies on the lower boundary slice of Dvm.
Figure 23 depicts four w-matching segments illustrating Definition 7.3.

vm. For i ∈[2, n], there are

Note also that every w-matching path in Definition 7.1 arises uniquely as the composite of matching
segments.

Remark 7.6. These are the schober local models categorifying the appearances of Dehn twists for
parallel transports in Picard-Lefschetz theory, which in the case of weave schobers are combinatorial-
ized in Section 7.3. To wit, Equations (83) and (84) encode the fact that, for a regular fiber fixed east
of the critical points, counterclockwise encircling a critical value leads to a positive twist along the
associated vanishing cycle. Specifically, the parametrized schober refines this information by declaring
that passing down to the right of a critical value is trivialized to be the identity, as in Equation (83),
and passing down to left of it results in the application of the positive twist associated to its vanishing
cycle, as in Equation (84).

7.2
Weave vanishing paths and transport
Let w : β →δ(β) be a Demazure weave. We now introduce a certain type of w-matching path, as in
Definition 7.1, which will be used to construct the exceptional collection ∆w and its dual.
Definition 7.10 (Weave vanishing paths). Let p ∈w be a trivalent vertex. By definition:
65


## Page 66


Figure 24: A general Demazure weave with its weave vanishing paths, as introduced in
Definition 7.10. The left vanishing paths ℘l are depicted in pink, and the right vanishing
paths ℘r in yellow.
1. The right weave vanishing path ℘r(p) ⊆D is the embedded path given by concatenating the
straight horizontal path from p to the right, until w is entirely to the left of the path, with the
straight vertical path heading downwards, as in Figure 24 (yellow). The path ℘r starts as in
Figure 25.(2).
2. The left weave vanishing path ℘l(p) ⊆D is the embedded path given by concatenating the
following four paths. First, the horizontal path from p to the left starting with the upwards hook,
as in Figure 25.(1), until w is entirely to the right of the path. Second, the straight vertical path
heading downwards, until we descend to a height where the horizontal weave slice spells δ(β).
Third, the straight horizontal path to the right from that point, and fourth the straight vertical
path heading downwards. Left weave vanishing paths are depicted as in Figure 24 (pink).
Weave vanishing paths will typically be considered up to compactly supported planar isotopies in the
complement of the trivalent vertices of the weave, and assumed to be transversal to w.
Example 7.11. The w-matching path starting at vi, defined as the composition of the segments δ↓
vi
and δ⟳
vi−1, . . . , δ⟳
v1, is equivalent to the right weave vanishing path ℘r(pi). Similarly, the w-matching
path starting at vi given by the composition of δ↓
vi and δ⟲
vi−1, . . . , δ⟲
v1 gives a representative for ℘l(pi),
which will yield equivalent objects, up to a shift.
In order to illustrate the geometric idea that in this article w is being treated as a set of branch cuts,
we introduce the following notion:
Definition 7.12 (Weave transport). Let ℘be an oriented embedded segment in the plane transverse
to w. By definition, the w-transport of an object L ∈D along ℘is the object
Holw(L; ℘) ∈D

Si , and if ℘crosses a weave line decorated by si ∈W right-to-left, we apply the inverse

Figure 25: (1) The start of the path for a left vanishing path at the trivalent vertex of a

for parallel transport in the presence of a weave, cf. Definition 7.12 .
The combinatorial rules in Figure 25, and thus Definition 7.12, are consistent with the fact that
counter-clockwise winding around a trivalent vertex leads to a positive twist, as in Picard-Lefschetz
theory, cf. Figure 26.(1), Similarly, they imply that performing a zig-zaging as we cross a weave line,
as in Figure 26.(2), leads to the same transport as the straight segment in Figure 25.(4). In particular,
weave parallel transport is invariant under planar isotopies in the complement of the trivalent vertices
of the weave, and thus paths ℘will typically be considered up to such isotopies, see e.g. Definition 7.10.

(1) The standard thimbles (∆w

We conclude by recording how weave thimbles behave under weave equivalence and weave mutation.
By Theorem 3.8, the weave schober Fw remains invariant under weave equivalences except for the
case of a pulley move. Specifically, for a forward pulley move or a forward weave mutation from w to
w′, the weave schober changes according to Fw 7→µ#

Therefore, by construction, the possible changes of weave thimbles under weave equivalences and
weave mutations are the perverse schober analogues of Hurwitz moves for distinguished bases, see
e.g. [Sei08, Section (16d)]. Thus, if we have correctly developed the theory of weave schobers, one
expects weave thimbles to either remain invariant or change by mutation of exceptional collections.
This is indeed the case:

Proofs of Theorem B.(2), B.(3) and B.(4)
The aim of this section is to prove Parts (2), (3) and (4) for Theorem B. These results were also stated
as Theorems 4.6, 4.5 and 4.8, respectively.
8.1

8.2
Invariance of categorical Lusztig cycles under weave equivalences
Let us now establish Theorem B.(3), see also Theorem 4.5. In order to do so, we first prove Lemma 8.3
and Proposition 8.4, which will be used in the argument for Theorem B.(3).
Let w be a Demazure weave with m trivalent vertices and Gw its associated graph. Given i ∈[1, m],
consider the subgraph Gw,≥i ⊂Gw form by the vertices vi, . . . , vm and all their incident edges. In
particular, the edge ei becomes external in Gw,≥i. Such a subgraph specifies the corresponding exit
path subcategory Exit(Gw,≥i) ⊂Exit(Gw). The weave schober Fw for w, which is a functor with
domain Exit(Gw), can consequently be restricted to this subcategory: the restriction of Fw along
Exit(Gw,≥i) ⊂Exit(Gw) will be denoted by Fw,≥i.
Lemma 8.3. Let w be a Demazure weave and i ∈[1, m]. Then the following holds:

Proof of Theorem B.(3). To ease notation, we omit the equivalence of global sections of the weave
schobers induced by the given weave equivalence w ∼w′, cf. Theorem 3.8.(3). By decomposing the
given weave equivalence w ≃w′ into height-preserving weave equivalences and pulley moves, it suf-
fices to prove the assertion for these two types of weave equivalences. In the former case, if the weave
equivalence w ∼w′ is height-preserving, Lemma 7.22 implies that there is an identification of their
standard weave thimbles ∆w
i ≃∆w′
i
for all i ∈[1, m]. Hence, by construction, the objects of the simple
minded collections Sw
i ≃Sw′
i
are equivalent for all i ∈[1, m]. Theorem 8.2 thus implies that Lw
i ≃Lw′
i
for all i ∈[1, m], concluding Theorem B.(3) for height-preserving equivalences. The case of a pulley
move, which is more subtle, is proven as follows.
Consider a pulley move w →w′ exchanging the heights of the vertices pi, pi+1, for some i ∈[1, m].
Swapping w and w′ if necessary, we can and do assume that the weave equivalence is a backward
pulley move from w to w′. As above, we ease notation by omitting w and w′, keeping the prime as
an exponent in the latter case. Since Lemma 7.22 implies that ∆k ≃∆

k for all k ∈[1, i), we certainly
have Sk ≃S

8.3
Tilting of categorical Lusztig cycles under weave mutation
Let us now prove Theorem B.(4), cf. also Theorem 4.8. At a technical level, an important difference
in the following proof of Theorem B.(4), in contrast to the proof of Theorem B.(3) in Section 8.2, is
that equivalence (117) does not hold for a weave mutation. In a nutshell, under a weave mutation,
the difference between the two terms in (117) is non-trivial and, as we will now establish, leads to the
simple-minded collection of categorical Lusztig cycles undergoing a mutation.

9
Silting objects and their weave cycles
In this section we conclude our results on categorical weave calculus by studying two silting collections
{Pw
i } and {
P
w
i }, respectively left and right dual to the simple-minded categorical Lusztig cycles {Lw
i }.
The goal is to describe the objects of these silting collections in terms of both the weave w and its
perverse schober Fw. In order to achieve this, we first prove a type of Ringel duality for weave schobers
in Section 9.1, cf. Proposition 9.5. The silting collections are then introduced in Section 9.2, and we
then use such duality to establish the desired characterizations in Section 9.3, cf. Theorem 9.13 and
Corollary 9.14.

9.3
Weave realization of dual categorical Lusztig cycles
The goal of this subsection is to describe the objects in the silting collections {P↓
i } and {
P
↓
i } associated
to {∆w
i } in terms of the Demazure weave w itself. By Lemma 9.9 and Lemma 9.10, we can equivalently

Corollary 9.14, cf. also Construction 9.15.
Definition 9.12 (Weave cycles realization). Let w be a Demazure weave and X ∈RΓ(G↕
w, F↕
w) a
global section. By definition, X is said to be realized by a weave cycle ϑ : E(w) −→Z≥0 if:
1. ϑ satisfies the tropical Lusztig propagation rules at the 4 and 6-valent vertices of w.
2. For any point q ∈ei in the edge ei ∈G↕
w, the object X(ei) admits a (βq, aq)-filtration, where
(βq, aq) is the weighted braid word associated to ϑ at the horizontal slice of w at the height of q.
By definition, if X lies in RΓ(G↑
w, F↑
w) or RΓ(G↓
w, F↓
w), a weave cycle realization of X consists of a
weave cycle realization of its image in RΓ(G↕
w, F↕
w).
A motivating instance for Definition 9.12 is that the categorical Lusztig cycle Lw
i ∈RΓ(G↓
w, F↓
w) is
realized by the Lusztig cycle γi : E(w) −→Z≥0 from Definition 2.1, as is implied by our constructions
in Sections 4 and 5. In general, the downwards facing global section P↓
i of the dual silting collection
might not admit a weave cycle realization. Nevertheless, we will now establish that there is a natural
weave cycle realization of its upwards dual
P
↑
i :

9.4
Ancillary remarks on tilting theory and exceptional collections
Part of the results and constructions of this paper are inspired by and related to the theory of highest
weight abelian categories, cf. [CPS88] or Definition A.23. This final subsection summarizes a few key
aspects in the main text as they relate to this perspective.
Must of our results occur in the context of stable ∞-categories, or at least triangulated categories, in
contrast to that of abelian categories. Indeed, while abelian categories suffice to tackle reduced braid
words, cf. e.g. (32), Remark 5.2 and [GLS06,GLS08,BIRS09], the derived setting is required in order
to establish a theory for non-reduced positive braid words. Therefore, there is merit in suggesting a
potential candidate for the notion of a highest weight structure in the stable context. In a nutshell,
our constructions and results lead to the following potential tenet:
A stable k-linear ∞-category endowed with a full exceptional collection can be considered
as the stable analog of an abelian category endowed with a highest weight structure.
This tenet is analyzed in detail in Appendix A. First, in Appendix A.2, we associate a simple-minded
collection and a Koszul dual silting collection to a stable ∞-category with a full exceptional collection.
These collections are the stable analogs of the simple objects and projective objects in a highest weight
abelian category, respectively. Similarly to how the projective objects in a highest weight abelian cat-
egory arise via universal extensions of the standard objects, the silting collection arises via universal
(possibly higher) extensions between the standard objects by Proposition A.13. We also refer to The-
orem A.27 for a precise relationship with highest weight abelian categories.
Second, highest weight abelian categories admit so-called tilting objects, which are objects character-
ized by admitting both a standard and a costandard flag. A stable version of the tilting object, which
97


## Page 98


is both a higher extension of the standard exceptional objects and a higher extension of the costan-
dard exceptional objects, appears in Lemma 9.10. In the theory of highest weight abelian categories,
tilting objects are used to construct the Ringel duality functors, which are functors from a highest
weight abelian category to its Ringel dual. Within the formalism of perverse schobers, we presented
an abstract construction of two Ringel-type duality functors in Lemma 9.2.(3). They intertwine the
standard and costandard exceptional collections, cf. Proposition 9.5, as well the silting object and the
stable version of tilting object, cf. Lemma 9.9. A difference with respect to classical Ringel duality
is that the definitions of these functors are not module-theoretical and rather in terms of perverse
schobers.

Remark 9.17. One can show that any proper k-linear stable ∞-category C with a full exceptional
collection ∆1, . . . , ∆m ∈C arises formally as the ∞-category of global sections of a perverse schober
parametrized by a linear ribbon graph with one external edge, as follows. First, there is a functor
C →ˆC from C into the proper 2-Calabi–Yau completion ˆC of C, mapping ∆i to a 2-spherical object
Xi. Then, we can use the ordered collection of spherical objects X1, . . . , Xm ∈ˆC to define a perverse
schober F, in line with Definition 3.3. The ∞-category of global sections of F is then equivalent to
C in such a manner that the ith exceptional object ∆i is identified with the ith standard exceptional
object defined using left induction as in Equation (89). See also [Chr26a] for more details, and note
that this passage to the Calabi–Yau completion can be seen as an algebraic version of the passage to
the double branched cover of a Lefschetz fibration in [Sei08, Section (18a)].
Each of the notions of an exceptional, a simple-minded, or a silting collection admits a notion of
mutation, cf. Appendix A.1. For the latter two, these are part of tilting and silting theory, respectively.
Now, a central result in the study of the tilting theory of simple-minded collections and the silting
theory of silting collections is that Koszul duality commutes with these respective notions of mutation,
see Theorem A.9. The relation between mutations of a given full exceptional collection and mutations
of the corresponding simple-minded collection, or the corresponding silting collection, is more subtle,
cf. Theorem 4.5 and Theorem 4.8. Based on these two results in our main text, we now present two
98


## Page 99


sufficiency homological criteria ensuring that an exceptional mutation induces either a simple-minded
mutation, or that it induces no mutation of the corresponding simple-minded collection.
Theorem 9.18. Let C be a proper k-linear stable ∞-category with a full exceptional collection ∆1, . . . , ∆m,
and i ∈[1, m] a fixed index. Suppose that

a linear graph with m vertices and one external edge arising from a collection of m many 2-spherical

## Page 100


A
Exceptional, simple-minded, and silting collections in enhanced
triangulated categories
The object of this appendix is to study simple-minded and silting collection in k-linear stable ∞-
categories arising from full exceptional collections. Specifically, in Appendix A.1 we recall the notions
of exceptional collections, simple-minded collections, and silting collections, as well as their corre-
sponding mutations. In Appendix A.2, we describe how a full exceptional collection in a k-linear
stable ∞-category gives rise to Koszul dual simple-minded and silting collections. In Appendix A.3,
we discuss how our constructions generalize aspects of the theory of highest weight abelian categories.
We refer to Section 9.4 for a discussion on how the constructions in the main part of the manuscript
relate to those in this appendix and the theory of highest weight abelian categories.
Throughout this section, we formulate all notions and statements in the setting of k-linear ∞-
categories, to keep the notation consistent with the main text. The results can equally be formulated
on the level of the k-linear triangulated homotopy 1-categories13 However, some of the proofs make
use of the enhancement, specifically when they appeal to Koszul duality, see Theorem A.9.
A.1
Recollections
The objects of interest in this appendix are defined as follows.
Definition A.1. Let C be a small k-linear stable ∞-category.
(1) A finite sequence of objects ∆1, . . . , ∆m in C is called an exceptional collection if
• MorD(∆i, ∆i) ≃k for all 1 ≤i ≤m, and
• MorD(∆i, ∆j) ≃0 for all 1 ≤j < i ≤m.
The exceptional collection is said to be a full exceptional collection if in addition
• the object Lm
i=1 ∆i generates C under finite limits and colimits.
(2) A finite sequence of objects ∇1, . . . , ∇m in C is called a (full) co-exceptional collection if ∇m, . . . , ∇1
is a (full) exceptional collection.
(3) A full exceptional collection ∆1, . . . , ∆m and a full co-exceptional collection ∇m, . . . , ∇1 in C are
said to be dual to each other if

For Definition A.1.(6), we remark that a simple-minded collection is uniquely determined by being

Remark A.2. (1) Recall that a small k-linear stable ∞-category C is called proper if, for all objects
X, Y ∈C, the derived Hom object MorC(X, Y ) ∈D(k) has finite dimensional total homology,
i.e. lies in Dperf(k). In this appendix, we will only study full exceptional collections in proper
k-linear stable ∞-categories.
(2) One can show that if a proper k-linear stable ∞-category admits a full exceptional collection, then
it is also idempotent complete and smooth.
Exceptional collections, simple-minded collections and silting collections each admits a notion of mu-
tation, as follows. (See also [KY14, Section 7].) We begin by recalling the mutation of exceptional
collections, which requires the notion of a twist.
Definition A.3 (Twists). Let C be a proper k-linear stable ∞-category and ∆i−1, ∆i, ∆i+1 ∈C.
(1) The positive twist of ∆i by ∆i−1 is defined as

For mutations of simple-minded collections, we proceed as follows. First, fixing a simple-minded
collection S1, . . . , Sm in C in a k-linear stable ∞-category, it determines a bounded t-structure on C
whose heart H is a finite length abelian category, see for instance [Sch, Theorem 4.4]. Let us assume
that the trianguled homotopy category of C is also Hom-finite and Krull–Schmidt. Then the object Si
in the simple-minded collection determines torsion pairs14 ⟨add(Si), T⟩and ⟨F, add(Si)⟩, with add(Si)
the additive closure of Si, see [Dug15, Theorem 3.3].
The forward tilt H♯of H at add(Si) is defined as the abelian subcategory of C arising from the torsion
pair ⟨T, add(Si)[1]⟩. Similarly, the backward tilt H♭of H is defined as the abelian subcategory of C
arising from the torsion pair ⟨add(Si)[−1], F⟩.
Definition A.6 (Simple-minded mutation). Let C be a k-linear stable ∞-category whose triangulated
homotopy category is Hom-finite and Krull–Schmidt. Let S1, . . . , Sm be a simple-minded collection
in C describing the simple objects in a heart H ⊂C. The forward mutation of S1, . . . , Sm is defined
as the collection of simple objects in the forward tilt H♯, considered as objects of C. The backwards
mutation of S1, . . . , Sm is similarly defined as the simple objects in the backward tilt H♭.
The following result provides a more explicit description of the tilts at an object Si ∈C of a simple-
minded collection, as in Definition A.6, in the case that the object Si is itself rigid.
Proposition A.7 ([KQ15, Prop. 5.4]). Let C be a k-linear stable ∞-category whose triangulated
homotopy category is Hom-finite and Krull–Schmidt and {Sj}1≤j≤m a simple-minded collection in C.

The relation between these collections and their mutations above can be summarized as follows:
Theorem A.9 (Koszul-duality [KN,KY14,SY19]). Let A be a connective dg algebra. Assume that the
total homology of A is finite dimensional or that A is smooth and that Hi(A) is finite dimensional for
all i ≥0. Then there is bijective correspondence (called Koszul duality) between equivalence classes of
(1) silting collections in Dperf(A),
(2) simple-minded collections in Dfin(A), and
(3) bounded t-structures on Dfin(A) with length heart.
Further, the bijection between (1) and (2) commutes with mutations, and the corresponding simple-
minded collections are Koszul dual to the corresponding silting collections.
Note that if A in Theorem A.9 is finite dimensional and smooth, then Dperf(A) = Dfin(A).
A.2
From exceptional collections to simple-minded and silting collections

exceptional collection. For that, we will use the following notion of semi-twists.

For part (1), the objects {Pi}1≤i≤m stably generate the exceptional collection, hence they also stably
generate C. We will now show that Mor(Pi, Pj) is connective by an induction on the length m of the
exceptional collection.
For the base case m = 1, P1 = ∆1 is exceptional and hence a silting object. For the induction

must be connective, because the outer terms are as well.
Thus MorC(Pj, P1) is connective for all

The indecomposablity of Pi follows from a similar
argument as the proof of the indecomposability of Si in Proposition A.12.
Remark A.14. Note that, in contrast to Proposition A.12, cf. Lemma A.22, the two silting collections
in Proposition A.13 are typically different. This is a stable ∞-category analogue of the fact that
the collections of projective and injective objects in the setting of abelian categories are typically
different.
Let us now show in Theorem A.21 that the simple-minded collection in Proposition A.12, and the silt-
ing collection in Proposition A.13, each associated with the same full exceptional collection, are Koszul
dual to each other. For that, we compare the associated t-structures. The argument for Lemma A.22
will similarly use t-structures.
Note that a t-structure on a stable ∞-category is by definition a t-structure on the triangulated homo-
topy category, see for instance [Lur17, Def. 1.2.1.4]. All t-structures will be homological, meaning that
the positive aisle is stable under suspension. First, we recall how to glue t-structure via recollement:

canonical inclusions of ⟨∆1, . . . , ∆m−1⟩= ⟨∇1, . . . , ∇m−1⟩, which coincide by the inductive hypothesis.
Since passing to opposite categories exchanges left and right adjoints, we thus also have (ˆi!)op ≃i∗.
Furthermore, under the equivalence ⟨∇m⟩≃Dperf(k) ≃⟨∆m⟩, the functor (ˆj∗)op identifies with j∗,
since it is given by the Verdier quotient functor by i∗.
Since passing to opposite categories exchanges the left and right aisles, an object X ∈⟨∇1, . . . , ∇m⟩
lies in the positive aisle if and only if
i!(X) ≃(ˆi!)op(X) ∈(⟨∇1, . . . , ∇m−1⟩op
≤0)op = ⟨∆1, . . . , ∆m−1⟩≥0
and
j∗(X) ≃(ˆj∗)op(X) ∈(⟨∇m⟩op
≤0)op = ⟨∆m⟩≥0 .
Thus, the two t-structures coincide.
A.3
Relation with highest weight theory
Highest weight categories were introduced in [CPS88]. The following definition uses a slightly different
formulation given in [Kra17], which itself refers to [Rou08]. We will further only consider highest weight
categories whose poset is a totally ordered set on m elements. The full highest weight theory for more
general, possibly infinite, posets can be found in [BS24].
Definition A.23 (Highest weight structure). Let A be a k-linear abelian category having only finitely
many isoclasses of simple objects and finite dimensional Homs.
A highest weight structure on A
consists of a collection of objects ∆1, . . . , ∆m ∈A, satisfying the following conditions:
• HomA(∆i, ∆i) ≃k for all 1 ≤i ≤m.
• HomA(∆i, ∆j) = 0 for all 1 ≤j < i ≤m.
• For all 1 ≤i ≤m, there exists a projective cover Pi →∆i whose kernel has a filtration whose
associated graded consists of objects {∆j}i<j≤m.
• The object Lm
i=1 Pi is a projective generator of A, meaning that an object X ∈A vanishes if
and only if HomA(Lm
i=1 Pi, X) = 0.
If an abelian category A has a highest weight structure, then the images of the objects ∆1, . . . , ∆m
under the inclusion N(A) ⊂Db(A), with N(A) the nerve of A, form a full exceptional collection, see
for instance [Kra17, Thm. 5.2]. These objects are called the standard objects in A. Furthermore, we
can label the iso-classes of simple objects in A by S1, . . . , Sm, and for all i ∈[1, m] there exists an
epimorphism ∆i →Si in A whose kernel has composition factors in {Si}1≤j<i.
Remark A.24. A highest weight structure, as in Definition A.23, can equivalently be described as a
quasi-hereditary structure on the endomorphism algebra of the projective generator, cf. [CPS88].
110


## Page 111


The results of Appendix A.2 in the stable setting are inspired by the notion of a highest weight
abelian category. The full exceptional collection acquires the role of the standard objects, the associ-
ated simple-minded collection has the role of the simple objects, and the associated silting collection
has the role of the projective objects. In the following results, we elucidate the precise relationship
between the constructions from Appendix A.2 and the theory of highest weight categories.
Lemma A.25. Let A be a k-linear abelian category equipped with a highest weight structure with stan-
dard objects ∆1, . . . , ∆m ∈A. Then the silting collection {Pi}1≤i≤m in Db(A) from Proposition A.13
associated with the full exceptional collection ∆1, . . . , ∆m ∈Db(A) describes the image of the collection
of projective A-modules {Pi}1≤i≤m under the inclusion N(A) ⊂Db(A).
Proof. Let us denote Pj,i := T −,>0

be the abelian subcategory generated by ∆1, . . . , ∆j. Let us prove by induction on j that {Pj,i}1≤i≤j
describes the images of the projectives in A≤j in Db(A≤j) ⊂Db(A). The base case j = 1 readily holds.
For the inductive step, we proceed as follows. First, the projective objects in a highest weight abelian
category arise via iterated maximal extensions between the standard objects. Indeed, this follows for
instance from the construction of the tilting modules described in the proof of [Soe99, Proposition 3.1]
applied to the Ringel dual highest weight category. Alternatively, a direct construction can also be
found in [Kra22, Ex. 8.1.10], where these maximal extensions are called universal extensions. Now,
since A≤j+1 is highest weight, the projective objects in A≤j+1 arise from the projective objects in A≤j
by universal extension with add(∆j+1), as well as the object ∆j+1 itself. Since Pj,i is projective in
A≤j, the Statement 1 in [DR89, Appendix] implies that
Extl(Pj,i, ∆j+1) ≃0,

Thus, τ>0 Mor(Pj,i, ∆j+1)∗is concentrated in degree 1. This shows that Pj+1,i = T −,>0

Lemma A.26. Let A be a k-linear abelian category equipped with a highest weight structure, and
standard objects ∆1, . . . , ∆m ∈A. Then the simple-minded collection {Sj}1≤j≤min Db(A) from Propo-
sition A.12 associated with the full exceptional collection ∆1, . . . , ∆m coincides with the image of the
collection of simple modules {Sj}1≤j≤m in A under the inclusion N(A) ⊂Db(A).
Proof. This follows from combining Theorem A.9, Theorem A.21 and Lemma A.25.

collection ∆1, . . . , ∆m, we denote as above

coincides with the image of the projective objects in A≤j in Db(A≤j). Thus, the derived endomorphism
algebra is concentrated in degree 0, showing that (1) holds.
Remark A.28. Let A′ be an abelian category and ∆1, . . . , ∆m ∈A′ a collection of objects giving
rise to a full exceptional collection in Db(A′). Then [Kra17, Theorem 5.2] gives a further homological
criterion for when these objects describe the standard objects in a highest weight structure on the
module category of the associated silting object: this is the case if and only if the exceptional collection
is strictly full, which coarsely means that the higher extension groups in the exact category of objects
filtered by ∆1, . . . , ∆m agree with those in Db(A′).
B
Coherent chain complexes and grafting
The goal of this appendix is to introduce bounded coherent chain complexes in stable ∞-categories,
define their totalization, and describe their grafting. In the main text, we will repeatedly use grafting
of coherent chain complexes, as described in Lemma B.8. Let us denote I := N({1 →0}) ≃(∆1)op.
Definition B.1. Let D be a stable ∞-category and n ≥0.
(1) An n-cube in D is a functor In →D. We denote by Cuben(D) := Fun(In, D) the ∞-category of
n-cubes in D.
